# A Unified Theory of Sparsification 

Sanjeev Khanna ∗ Aaron Putterman † Madhu Sudan ‡

July 20, 2026 

Abstract 

We study the sparsifiability of real-valued codes , a unifying abstraction that generalizes both combinatorial and continuous notions of sparsification, including spectral sparsification. In our setting, a code C ⊆ Rm 

> ≥0

is simply a collection of nonnegative real-valued vectors, and for a parameter ϵ > 0, a (1 ± ϵ)-sparsifier of C is a subset T ⊆ [m], together with weights w ∈ RT

> ≥0

,such that, for every c ∈ C, P 

> i∈T

wici ∈ (1 ± ϵ) Pmi=1 ci. When C ⊆ { 0, 1}m, this specializes to code sparsification, and hence captures CSP sparsification, as studied by Khanna–Putterman– Sudan (SODA 2024, STOC 2025) and Brakensiek–Guruswami (STOC 2025). Similarly, for a graph G = ( V, E ), if one defines C = {c(x) : x ∈ RV } ⊆ RE 

> ≥0

by c(x)(u,v ) = ( xu − xv )2, then sparsifying C is exactly spectral graph sparsification, as studied by Spielman–Teng (SICOMP 2011). Although the techniques driving combinatorial and continuous sparsification have tradition-ally been largely disjoint, our main result is a single structural theorem governing the sparsi-fiability of arbitrary real-valued codes C ⊆ Rm

> ≥0

. The central parameter is continuous-valued non-redundancy (CVNRD), a real-valued analogue of non-redundancy that captures the largest approximately block-diagonal obstruction contained in C. Our theorem gives sparsifiers of size nearly-linear in CVNRD, and shows that CVNRD is also a lower-bound obstruction for the broad class of coordinate-wise unbiased randomized sparsification schemes. In the course of proving this result, we rely on new techniques; in particular, we completely forego the use of Gilmer’s entropy method, matrix Chernoff concentration, or Talagrand’s theory of chaining, and instead reveal the Sauer-Shelah lemma, its continuous relatives, and several extensions that we introduce, as the common combinatorial core for our sparsification results. As consequences of our main theorem, we obtain: (1) the first near-linear sized sparisfiers for higher powers of graph and hypergraph spectra, (2) nearly tight bounds for sparsifying sums of bounded sub-modular functions, (3) tight structural characterizations of sparsifiability of valued CSPs and (4) A new proof bypassing Gilmer’s entropy method, of Brakensiek and Guruswami’s theorem (STOC 2025) that non-redundancy characterizes sparsification of {0, 1}-valued codes.

Given a code C ⊆ Rm 

> ≥0

and an accuracy parameter ϵ > 0, the problem of real-valued code sparsi-fication asks for a small reweighted subset of coordinates that preserves the total weight of every codeword in C up to a (1 ± ϵ) factor. In this work, we give a structural characterization of the sparsifiability of arbitrary real-valued codes. 

1.1 Background 

Sparsification is the task of selecting and possibly reweighting a small substructure of a large object so that it preserves a prescribed family of measurements on the original object. A foundational example is the cut sparsification theorem of Bencz´ ur and Karger [BK96], which shows that every weighted graph admits a sparse reweighted subgraph that approximately preserves the value of every cut. This theorem has become a cornerstone of graph algorithms and combinatorial sparsification. Since this seminal work, a central question has been to understand which objects are sparsifiable, and which measurements can be preserved by a small reweighted substructure. A sequence of works by Spielman and Teng [ST11], Spielman and Srivastava [SS08], and Batson, Spielman, and Srivastava [BSS09] extended the cut-sparsification paradigm to spectral sparsification , where one preserves not only all cuts but the entire Laplacian quadratic form of the graph. Subsequent work has pushed sparsification well beyond graphs. For hypergraphs, results of Kogan and Krauthgamer [KK15], Chen, Khanna, and Nagda [CKN20], and Jambulapati, Lee, Liu, and Sidford [Lee23, JLS23, JLLS23] show that both cut and spectral notions admit sparsi-fiers with only eO(|V |/ϵ 2) hyperedges. 1 Related notions of sparsification have also been studied for constraint satisfaction problems (CSPs) [KK15, FK17, BZ20, KPS24a, KPS25, BG25], with [BG25] revealing surprising connections between CSP sparsification and classical questions in CSP ap-proximation [BCK20, CJP20, LW20, Car22, BGJ +25]. Sparsification has also been developed for families of positive semidefinite (PSD) matrices [dCSHS16, BKLM26, HLM +26, BBP26, BBKP26]. Together, these results suggest that sparsification is not merely a graph-theoretic phenomenon, but a structural principle appearing across combinatorics, optimization, and algebra. 

From Graphs to Codes. A more recent viewpoint recasts graph and hypergraph sparsification as special cases of sparsifying codes . Here a code C ⊆ { 0, 1}m is simply a collection of {0, 1}-valued vectors; we do not assume that it is linear unless explicitly stated. Equivalently, one may view C

as an m × | C| matrix whose columns are the codewords and whose rows are the coordinates . For an accuracy parameter ϵ ∈ (0 , 1), a (1 ± ϵ)-sparsifier of C is a small set of coordinates T ⊆ [m], together with weights wi ≥ 0 for i ∈ T , such that for every codeword c ∈ C,

X

> i∈T

wici ∈ (1 ± ϵ) · X

> i∈[m]

ci.

Thus, sparsifying a code means preserving the Hamming weight of every codeword using only a small reweighted subset of coordinates. This abstraction already contains classical graph cut sparsification. Given a graph G = ( V, E ), its cut code Ccut ⊆ { 0, 1}E has one codeword for each cut S ⊆ V , namely the indicator vector of the edges crossing S. Preserving the weight of every codeword in this code is exactly the same as preserving the value of every cut. Khanna, Putterman, and Sudan [KPS24a, KPS25] initiated a  

> 1Throughout this paper, we use eO(·) to hide factors of polylog( ·).

1systematic study of code sparsification, focusing on linear codes over F2.2 They proved that every such code admits a (1 ± ϵ)-sparsifier using a number of coordinates nearly linear in its dimension. Since the cut code of a graph is linear and has dimension at most |V | − 1, their theorem recovers the Bencz´ ur–Karger sparsification theorem as a corollary. Brakensiek and Guruswami [BG25] extended this theory from linear codes to arbitrary, poten-tially non-linear, binary codes. Their characterization is governed by a structural parameter called 

non-redundancy (NRD). 

Definition 1.1. For a code C ⊆ { 0, 1}m, NRD( C) is the largest size of a set I ⊆ [m] such that for each i ∈ I, there is a codeword c ∈ C with ci = 1 and cj = 0 for all j ∈ I \ { i}.Equivalently, NRD( C) is the size of the largest diagonal submatrix that appears in the matrix representation of C, up to permuting rows and columns. It measures how many coordinates can be forced to behave independently across codewords. Brakensiek and Guruswami proved that every code C ⊆ { 0, 1}m admits a (1 ± ϵ)-sparsifier with at most eO(NRD( C)/ϵ 2) coordinates. This is essentially tight: for a linear code, NRD( C) equals its dimension, recovering the bounds of [KPS24a, KPS25]; for graph cut codes, NRD corresponds to the size of a spanning forest, and is therefore at most |V | − 1. The tightness is also visible from the simplest obstruction: a diagonal code cannot be sparsified. Indeed, deleting any coordinate of an identity matrix turns the corresponding codeword from weight 1 to weight 0. Thus, for {0, 1}-valued codes, non-redundancy captures the fundamental obstruction to sparsification up to the usual logarithmic and ϵ-dependent factors. In particular, [BG25] shows that eO(NRD( C)/ϵ 2) ≥ max  

> T⊆[m]

{minimum sparsifier size of C|T } ≥ NRD( C),

where C|T = {c|T : c ∈ C} ⊆ { 0, 1}T .

Beyond Boolean Codes. Motivated by the sharp characterization of {0, 1}-valued code spar-sification, we now turn to the central problem of this paper: real-valued code sparsification . A

real-valued code is an arbitrary set C ⊆ Rm

> ≥0

, and for an accuracy parameter ϵ > 0, a (1 ± ϵ)-sparsifier of C is a small reweighted subset of coordinates T ⊆ [m], with weights wi ≥ 0 for i ∈ T ,such that for every c ∈ C, X

> i∈[m]

Thus, real-valued code sparsification strictly generalizes the Boolean setting. The challenge is to understand which structural features of a real-valued code govern the minimum possible size of such a sparsifier. Real-valued code sparsification captures continuous sparsification problems whose objective values depend on magnitudes, not merely on supports. For example, fix a graph G = ( V, E ) and a parameter p ≥ 1. Define a code CG ⊆ RE 

> ≥0

with one codeword for each vector x ∈ RV , where the codeword c(x) is given by 

c(x)(u,v ) = |xu − xv|p.

The weight of this codeword is exactly the p-energy of x on the graph:

In this sense, spectral sparsification is in fact a special case of real-valued code sparsification. More generally, suppose we are given nonnegative functions f1, . . . , f m : Rn → R≥0. The problem of sparsifying a sum of functions asks for a small set T ⊆ [m] and weights w : T → R≥0

This formulation encompasses spectral hypergraph sparsification [SY19,KKTY21b,KKTY21a,JLS23, Lee23], sparsification of sums of norms [JLLS23], sparsification of submodular functions [KK23, KˇZ23a, KZ23b, KPS24b], sparsification of discrete codes and CSPs [KPS24a, KPS25, BG25], and 

sparsification of hypergraphs with general splitting functions [VBK20, VBK21, VBK22]. 

Barriers to Real-Valued Sparsification. As observed in [BG25], real-valued codes have ob-structions to sparsification that do not arise for {0, 1}-valued codes. Consider the full code C =

{1, 2}m. For ϵ = 1 /8, every sparsifier of C must retain Ω( m) coordinates. Indeed, suppose a spar-sifier only looks at a set S ⊆ [m] with |S| < m/ 2. Then the codewords c = 1 m and c′ defined by 

c′ 

> i

= 1 for i ∈ S and c′ 

> i

= 2 for i / ∈ S are indistinguishable to the sparsifier, since they agree on every retained coordinate. However, 

X

> i

ci = m whereas X

> i

c′ 

> i

≥ 3m/ 2,

so no single reported value can approximate both within a (1 ± 1/8) factor. This obstruction cannot be detected from the Boolean support pattern of the code: every codeword in {1, 2}m has full support, so replacing each nonzero coordinate by 1 collapses the entire code to the single word 1m.This example reveals a fundamental difference between Boolean and real-valued sparsification: in real-valued codes, a witness to unsparsifiability may need to contain exponentially many code-words. Indeed, for any finite subcode C′ ⊆ { 1, 2}m, a standard sampling-and-union-bound argu-ment gives a sparsifier with roughly log |C′|/ϵ 2 coordinates. Consequently, any finite subcode that witnesses the Ω( m) lower bound for the full code C = {1, 2}m must have size 2 Ω( m).This behavior sharply contrasts with the {0, 1}-valued setting. There, [BG25] shows that every obstruction to sparsification has a concise diagonal witness : one can collect a small set of coordinates 3and codewords that form a diagonal matrix. For real-valued codes, such a small witness cannot exist in general. Thus, the right goal is not to find a short list of codewords witnessing every obstruction, but rather to find a concise structural language for reasoning about these exponentially large witnesses. A second barrier is that previous works on code sparsification [KPS24a,KPS25,BG25] operate in 

discrete settings with finitely many codewords. Their sparsification theorems rely on decomposition arguments: one peels off a small set of coordinates until the remaining code has sufficiently few relevant patterns, after which Chernoff bounds and union bounds show that random sampling succeeds. In the setting of real-valued code sparsification , however, the code may be infinite. This already occurs in spectral graph sparsification , where the codewords are indexed by all vectors 

x ∈ RV . Consequently, the sparsifiability of real-valued codes cannot be understood by counting codewords alone. Together, these barriers motivate the central question of this work: 

What governs the sparsifiability of real-valued codes? 

Our main contributions are (1) a structural characterization of these obstructions, and hence of the sparsifiability of arbitrary real-valued codes and (2) applications of this characterization to get new nearly-tight results in sparsification while also giving new proofs of some old sparsification results. 

1.2 Our Contributions 

In this subsection we first define a general structural parameter that we associate with continuous-valued codes (Definition 1.5) and then state a theorem showing that this parameter upper bounds sparsification with nearly tight lower bounds for the common approach to sparsification (Theo-rem 1.1). We then state some applications in Section 1.2.2. Then in Section 1.2.3 we specialize this definition to codes over alphabets of “bounded aspect ratio” (where the smallest and largest non-zero values in the code are bounded by a constant factor, see Definition 1.6). We state our the-orem which gives nearly-tight upper and lower bounds on sparsification in terms of this parameter (Theorem 1.4). We then state applications of this tighter characterization in Section 1.2.4. Before stating our results, we first make precise the sparsification quantities that we characterize. Recall that our goal is to understand the sparsifiability of arbitrary codes C ⊆ Rm

> ≥0

. Throughout the paper, we assume without loss of generality that C is closed under positive scaling: if c ∈ C

and λ > 0, then λc ∈ C. This assumption does not change the sparsification problem, since any fixed set of reweighted coordinates that preserves the weight of c also preserves the weight of λc .Our main notion is the following unweighted version of sparsifiability. For S ⊆ [m], write 

C|S = {c|S : c ∈ C}.

Definition 1.2. For C ⊆ Rm 

> ≥0

and ϵ > 0, the unweighted sparsifiability of C with parameter ϵ,denoted US( C, ϵ ), is US( C, ϵ ) = max

Thus, US( C, ϵ ) measures the worst-case sparsifier size over all coordinate restrictions of C. This is the standard robustness requirement in sparsification: a code should be considered sparsifiable only if none of its coordinate projections hides a much harder sparsification instance. The adjective “unweighted” distinguishes this setting from the corresponding weighted version, where the input coordinates may already come with prescribed weights. 4Definition 1.3. For C ⊆ Rm

⟨c|T , w ⟩ ∈ (1 ± ϵ) · ⟨ c, w ′⟩.

We will also use a randomized variant, which captures the sampling-and-reweighting schemes that underlie most randomized sparsification results. 

Definition 1.4. For C ⊆ Rm 

> ≥0

and ϵ > 0, a (1 ± ϵ) random sparsifier of C is a distribution ∆ over weight functions w : [ m] → R≥0 such that: 1. for every coordinate i ∈ [m], the sampling scheme is unbiased :

Ew∼∆wi = 1; 2. with probability at least 1 − 1/m over w ∼ ∆, the sampled weight function w is a (1 ± ϵ)-sparsifier of C, i.e., mX

The size of the random sparsifier is

the expected number of coordinates receiving nonzero weight. The random sparsifiability of C with parameter ϵ is RS( C, ϵ ) = max  

> S⊆[m]

> ∆: (1 ±ϵ) random sparsifier of C|S

Ew∼∆|Supp( w)|.

The unbiasedness condition says that every coordinate has expected weight 1 under the sampling scheme. This is the usual normalization in randomized sparsification: coordinates may be sam-pled with different probabilities and reweighted differently, but their expected contribution remains unchanged. The vast majority of sampling-based sparsification results fit into this framework. A no-table exception is the deterministic spectral sparsifier of Batson, Spielman, and Srivastava [BSS09], and results derived from it. It is important to emphasize that a random sparsifier is not a uniform sampling scheme. Even in the common independent-sampling paradigm, the sampling probabilities may be highly nonuniform and chosen using structural information about the instance: edge strengths in cut sparsification, effective resistances in spectral graph sparsification, leverage scores in matrix sparsification, or sensitivities in more general settings. In such schemes, coordinate i is typically sampled with probability pi and assigned weight 1 /p i if retained, which satisfies the unbiasedness condition 

Ew∼∆wi = 1 .

Our definition is broader still: the distribution ∆ may be any distribution over nonnegative weight functions w : [ m] → R≥0. Thus, the sampled coordinates need not be independent, and the final weights may be chosen as an arbitrary function of the sampled set and of the code C, provided only that the coordinate-wise unbiasedness condition holds. 51.2.1 A Global Classification 

We now state our global classification theorem. The central parameter is a real-valued analogue of non-redundancy, which we call continuous-valued non-redundancy and denote by CVNRD. In-formally, CVNRD measures the largest block-diagonal obstruction inside a real-valued code: each block behaves like a complete code over two well-separated values, while the interaction between different blocks is small. For ease of notation, for disjoint sets A1, . . . , A p ⊆ [m], we write 

A̸ =i = [

Aj , wt( c|A̸ =i ) = X 

> ℓ∈A̸=i

cℓ.

That is, wt( c|A̸ =i ) is the total weight of c on the selected coordinate sets (viewed as blocks) other than Ai.

Definition 1.5. Let C ⊆ Rm

> ≥0

, and let ϵ, χ, ρ ∈ (0 , 1]. The continuous-valued non-redundancy 

of C with parameters ( ϵ, χ, ρ ), denoted CVNRD( C, ϵ, χ, ρ ), is the maximum integer ℓ for which there exist subcodes C1, . . . , C p ⊆ C and disjoint sets A1, . . . , A p ⊆ [m] satisfying the following conditions: 1. (Witness size)

b(i)2 = cj > 0.

3. (Complete shattering condition) For each i ∈ [p] with |Ai| ≥ 2, there exist values b(i)1 , b (i)2 ∈

R≥0 with

cj ∈ b(i)1 · [1 , 1 + ϵχ ] for all j ∈ B, 

and

4. (Small cumulative off-block weight) For every i ∈ [p] and every c ∈ Ci,wt( c|A̸ =i ) ≤ ρ · b(i)2 · | Ai|.

6Let us unpack this definition. A CVNRD witness should be thought of as an approximately 

block-diagonal family of subcodes. Within each selected coordinate set Ai, the subcode Ci behaves like a complete block : for every subset B ⊆ Ai, some codeword realizes the “low” value on B and the “high” value on Ai \ B. This is the real-valued analogue of a Boolean block containing all of 

{0, 1}Ai .The parameters ϵ and χ control two different aspects of this completeness condition. The parameter ϵ controls the required separation between the low and high levels: the condition 

b(i)1 < (1 − ϵ)b(i)2

ensures that the two levels are sufficiently distinct. For example, if the two intended levels are 1 and 2, then this separation condition holds for any ϵ < 1/2, but fails for ϵ ≥ 1/2. The parameter χ controls how tightly the realized entries must cluster around these two levels. For instance, suppose the high level is b(i)2 = 2. Then entries equal to 2 and 2 .02 can both be treated as realizations of the same high level only if 2.02 ∈ 2 · [1 , 1 + ϵχ ],

equivalently, only if ϵχ ≥ 0.01. Thus, smaller χ makes the notion of a complete block more rigid. Finally, ρ controls how block-diagonal the witness is: smaller ρ forces the off-block entries to be small, both in total weight and coordinate by coordinate, relative to the scale b(i)2 of the block. In typical applications, ϵ is tied to the target sparsification accuracy, while χ and ρ are chosen to be poly( ϵ, 1/ log m). With this notation, our main theorem is the following. 

Theorem 1.1. Let C ⊆ Rm 

> ≥0

and ϵ ∈ (0 , 1) be given, and set ϵ′ = ϵ4  

> 10 13 log 8(m)

. Then 

US( C, ϵ ) ≤ min  

> χ∈(0 ,1] , ρ ∈(0 ,1]

O

 CVNRD (C, ϵ ′, χ, ρ )

χ6ρ · poly(log( m), ϵ −1)



.

Conversely, 

RS( C, ϵ ) ≥ Ω



ϵ · CVNRD 



C, 10 ϵ, χ = 1100 log 2(m) , ρ = ϵ

100 log 2(m)

 

.

In words, Theorem 1.1 shows that the sparsifiability of an arbitrary real-valued code is controlled by its continuous-valued non-redundancy. The upper bound says that every code can be sparsified to within polynomial factors in ϵ−1, χ−1, ρ−1, and log m of its CVNRD. The lower bound says that CVNRD is also an obstruction: any unbiased distribution over reweighted coordinate subsets that sparsifies the code must have expected support size proportional to CVNRD, up to the stated parameters. This is a strong lower bound against randomized sparsification algorithms. As discussed above, the term “random” here does not mean uniform or independent sampling; it allows arbitrary nonuniform, correlated, and adaptively weighted sampling-and-reweighting schemes, subject only to coordinate-wise unbiasedness. The remaining gap is that, for arbitrary real-valued codes, our lower bound is proved for this broad randomized model rather than for all reweighted coordinate subsets. Equivalently, it is not known in full generality whether US( C, ϵ ) must always be at least Ω(RS( C, ϵ )). We conjecture that this gap is only an artifact of the proof, and that CVNRD is a lower bound for all sparsifiers. In the bounded-aspect-ratio settings considered later (in Section 1.2.3), this issue disappears: the analogous structural parameters give lower bounds against all sparsifiers. 7A key feature of Theorem 1.1 is that it pays no factor depending on log |C|. This is essential: the code C may be infinite, as in spectral graph sparsification, and even finite real-valued obstructions may require exponentially many codewords. In this sense, the theorem extends the central message of [BG25]: sparsifiability is governed not by the number of codewords, but by the largest structured obstruction contained in the code. Indeed, Theorem 1.1 already essentially recovers the main theorem of [BG25] for {0, 1}-valued codes, albeit with worse dependence on ϵ−1 and log m. To see why, let C ⊆ { 0, 1}m, and consider a largest witness 

A1, . . . , A p ⊆ [m], C1, . . . , C p ⊆ C

to CVNRD( C, ϵ ′, χ, ρ ) with ρ < 1. Since the entries of C are only 0 and 1, the two levels in each complete block must correspond to 0 and 1: informally, b(i)1 ≈ 0 and b(i)2 ≈ 1. Moreover, the entrywise off-block condition gives 

cj ≤ ρb (i)2 < 1 for all j ∈ A̸ =i

and every c ∈ Ci. Since the entries are Boolean, this forces cj = 0 on all off-block coordinates. Thus, in the Boolean setting, every CVNRD witness is actually a truly block-diagonal collection of complete Boolean blocks. Concretely, the resulting matrix of codewords and coordinates has the form 



{0, 1}A1 0 0 . . . 

0 {0, 1}A2 0 . . . 

0 0 {0, 1}A3 . . . 

... ... ... . . .

 ,

where rows are grouped by A1, A 2, A 3, . . . , and columns are grouped by the corresponding subcodes 

C1, C 2, C 3, . . . . From such a matrix, one can extract an identity submatrix of size 

X

> i

|Ai| = CVNRD( C, ϵ ′, χ, ρ ) : inside each complete block {0, 1}Ai , simply choose the codewords that form the identity on Ai.Therefore, for Boolean codes, NRD( C) ≥ CVNRD( C, ϵ ′, χ, ρ ).

Applying Theorem 1.1 then yields sparsifiers of size 

O NRD( C) · poly( ϵ−1, log m) ,

recovering the qualitative content of [BG25]. As a byproduct, this gives a new proof that non-redundancy characterizes sparsifiability of 

{0, 1}-valued codes, without using Gilmer’s entropy method. For clarity and sharper parameters, we also include in Section 3 a simpler self-contained proof specialized to {0, 1}-valued codes, matching the bounds of [BG25]. Together, Definition 1.5 and Theorem 1.1 identify the structural obstruction governing spar-sifiability in arbitrary real-valued codes. We next show that this perspective also leads to new sparsification results. We first discuss applications to stronger forms of graph and hypergraph sparsification, and then turn to bounded-aspect-ratio codes, which yield sharper characterizations and applications to submodular functions and valued constraint satisfaction problems. 81.2.2 Applications to Stronger Spectral Graph and Hypergraph Sparsification

As we explain in the technical overview Section 1.3.1, this is indeed the case. More interestingly, the CVNRD perspective avoids the usual spectral-sparsification toolkit, namely, effective resistances, leverage scores, eigenvalues, and matrix concentration, and instead reduces the existence of spectral sparsifiers to an elementary combinatorial statement about graphs. The main difficulty is that existing approaches rely either on matrix concentration, which is tailored to quadratic forms and therefore to the case t = 2, or on generic chaining, which again relies on a “smooth” structure in the sparsified functions. 1.2.3 Stronger Classifications With Bounded Aspect Ratio 

In many applications, the codes we wish to sparsify are not arbitrary subsets of Rm 

> ≥0

but have more structure such as a bounded-aspect-ratio : every nonzero entry lies in a bounded range. We capture this by considering codes C ⊆ ({0}∪ [1 , k ]) m, where k is typically constant. In this bounded-aspect-ratio setting, our general classification theorem admits a simpler and tighter form. 

Definition 1.6. Let C ⊆  {0} ∪ [1 , k ]m. The bounded-aspect ratio continuous non-redundancy of C with parameter ϵ, denoted by BACNRD( C, ϵ ) is the largest integer ℓ for which there exist disjoint 3 A1, . . . , A p ⊆ [m] and subcodes C1, . . . , C p ⊆ C such that: 1. (Witness Size) Ppi=1 |Ai| = ℓ.2. (Singleton Edge Case) If |Ai| = 1, then ( Ci)|Ai̸ = {0}.3. (Complete Block Shattering) If |Ai| ≥ 2, there exists γ ∈ [0 , k ]Ai such that for every B ⊆ Ai

there is c ∈ Ci with for b ∈ B : cb ≥ γb + ϵ, for b ∈ Ai \ B : cb ≤ γb − ϵ. 

4. (Zeros Off-Block) For every i̸ = j, ( Ci)|Aj = 0 Aj .The witness size, singleton case, and shattering condition parallel the corresponding conditions in Definition 1.5. The main simplification is the off-block condition: here the off-block entries are required to be exactly zero, rather than merely small in total weight and coordinate-wise magnitude as in CVNRD. With this cleaner obstruction in hand, we obtain a sharper characterization of sparsifiability in the bounded-aspect-ratio regime. Up to the displayed dependence on ϵ and polylogarithmic factors, BACNRD governs the optimal sparsifier size. 

Theorem 1.4. Let C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m with k = O(1) . Then, 1. There exists ϵ′ such that ϵ′ = Ω( ϵ) and US( C, ϵ ′) ≥ Ω( ϵ · BACNRD( C, ϵ )) .

10 2. For ϵ′ = ϵ 

> 256 log( m)

, US( C, ϵ ) ≤ eO(BACNRD( C, ϵ ′)/ϵ 4).

Thus, unlike in the fully general real-valued setting, the lower bound here applies to arbitrary sparsifiers, not only to randomized sampling-and-reweighting schemes. This is the sense in which bounded aspect ratio gives a stronger classification. When the input code is equipped with weights, there is an analogous parameter, the bounded-aspect-ratio continuous-valued chain length , which governs weighted sparsifiability . We defer these results to Section A. 

1.2.4 Applications of Bounded-Aspect Ratio Sparsification 

Finally, as applications of the bounded-aspect-ratio theory, we obtain sharper bounds for sparsifying sums of submodular functions and a first general characterization of the worst-case sparsifiability of valued constraint satisfaction problems (VCSPs) .4

Submodular Sparsification. In submodular sparsification , one is given submodular functions 

f1, . . . , f m : 2 [n] → R≥0. Recall that submodularity means that, for every i ∈ [m] and all sets 

A, B ⊆ [n], fi(A) + fi(B) ≥ fi(A ∪ B) + fi(A ∩ B). The goal is to find an ideally small set T ⊆ [m], together with weights w : T → R≥0, such that for every S ⊆ [n], 

X

> i∈T

w(i)fi(S) ∈ (1 ± ϵ) X

> i∈[m]

fi(S).

Despite attention in numerous works [KZ23b, KK23, JLLS23, KPS24b], the best known sparsifiers for arbitrary submodular functions have size O(n3/ϵ 2), due to Kenneth and Krauthgamer [KK23]. On the other hand, the best known lower bounds are Ω( n2), and it has remained open to pin down the complexity in this range. Using our bounded-aspect-ratio framework, we essentially settle this question for bounded submodular functions. 

Theorem 1.5. Let k = O(1) be an integer, and let f1, . . . , f m : 2 [n] → { 0, 1, . . . , k } be submodular functions. Then there is a set T ⊆ [m], together with weights w : T → R≥0, such that for every 

S ⊆ [n], X

> i∈T

w(i)fi(S) ∈ (1 ± ϵ) X

> i∈[m]

fi(S),

and |T | = eO(n2/ϵ 4).

The lower bound of Ω( n2) already holds for {0, 1}-valued submodular functions, via directed cut functions; see [KK23] for further discussion. Thus, Theorem 1.5 gives further evidence that n2 is perhaps the right sparsifier size for submodular functions. Extending these techniques from bounded to arbitrary nonnegative submodular functions, potentially using the full CVNRD classification theorem, remains a promising open direction. 

Valued CSP Sparsification Our results also give tight characterizations for the sparsifiability of valued CSPs . Fix a predicate P : Σ r → R≥0 and a universe of variables x1, . . . , x n ∈ Σ. An instance Ψ consists of constraints indexed by i ∈ [m], where the ith constraint applies P to a tuple 

Ti ∈  [n]

> r

 of variables and may be assigned a weight wi ≥ 0. For an assignment x ∈ Σn, the value 

> 4Equivalently, these may be viewed as hypergraphs equipped with arbitrary real-valued splitting functions.

11 of the instance is Ψ( x) = P 

> i∈[m]

wiP (x|Ti ). A sparsifier is a reweighted sub-instance bΨ such that, for every x ∈ Σn, bΨ( x) ∈ (1 ± ϵ)Ψ( x),

while retaining as few constraints as possible. This is exactly general hypergraph sparsification :variables are vertices, constraints are hyperedges, the predicate P is the splitting function, and the alphabet Σ specifies the number of parts in the partition; the standard cut case corresponds to 

|Σ| = 2. Following prior work on CSP sparsification [KK15,FK17,BZ20,KPS24a,KPS25,BG25,BGP25], we fix a predicate P of constant arity over a constant-size alphabet and ask for the worst-case size of a (1 ± ϵ)-sparsifier over all n-variable instances. We denote this quantity by SPR( P, n, ϵ ). While valued CSPs have appeared previously in real-valued sparsification [BZ20, BG25], concrete structural characterizations were known only for random instances [BGP25]. Most prior structural results focus on Boolean predicates. For P : Σ r → { 0, 1}, CSP sparsifica-tion coincides with {0, 1}-valued code sparsification: each assignment x ∈ Σn maps to the codeword 

c(x) ∈ { 0, 1}m with c(x)i = 1[P (x|Ti ) = 1]. For real-valued predicates, our framework suggests the following analogue of non-redundancy. 

Definition 1.7. Let P : Σ r → R≥0 and consider all n-variable instances Ψ with constraints on 

T1, . . . , T m. Define DNRD( P, n ) as the maximum ℓ such that there exist disjoint A1, . . . , A p ⊆ [m]and assignment families X1, . . . , X p ⊆ Σn with: 1. (Witness Size) Ppi=1 |Ai| = ℓ.2. ( Singletons ) If |Ai| = 1, then there exists x ∈ Xi with P (x|Tℓ )̸ = 0 for the unique ℓ ∈ Ai.3. ( Block-diagonal ) For every i̸ = j, for every x ∈ Xi and every ℓ ∈ Aj , P (x|Tℓ ) = 0. 4. ( Completeness ) If |Ai| ≥ 2, there exist b0̸ = b1 ∈ Im( P ) such that for every v ∈ { b0, b 1}Ai

there exists x ∈ Xi with P (x|Tℓ ) = vℓ for all ℓ ∈ Ai.Indeed, by Theorem 1.4, we can immediately conclude that DNRD( P, n ) is essentially equal (up to polylogarithmic factors) to the worst-case sparsifiability of VCSPs with predicate P .

Corollary 1.6. For every predicate P : Σ r → R≥0 and every ϵ ∈ (0 , κ ) for some κ = Ω(1) ,

Ω DNRD( P, n ) ≤ SPR( P, n, ϵ ) ≤ eO DNRD( P, n )/ϵ 4.

For constant ϵ, this corollary provides an exact analog of [BG25]: it reduces the study of VCSP sparsifier size to understanding the redundancy induced by the predicate P . In particular, when complete blocks are absent, VCSP sparsifiability coincides with that of the corresponding {0, 1}-valued CSP (and code). Interestingly, as we show in Section 7, these complete blocks arise even with very simple pred-icates. As an example, if we consider the predicate P : {0, 1}2 → { 0, 1, 2} introduced in the work of [BGP25], where: 

P (00) = 0 , P (01) = 0 , P (10) = 1 , P (11) = 2 ,

we in fact show that DNRD( P, n ) = Ω( n2), as we are able to construct Ω( n) disjoint, complete blocks of size Ω( n). For comparison, if one looks only at the “zero vs. non-zero” structure of P ,one gets the predicate ˆP : {0, 1}2 → { 0, 1} such that ˆP (00) = 0 , ˆP (01) = 0 , ˆP (10) = 1 , ˆP (11) = 1 .

12 Such a predicate is known to have NRD( ˆP , n ) = O(n)! This shows that, despite potentially seeming unnatural, these notions of “complete blocks” are in fact inherent to the study of VCSPs. Finally, chain length and non-redundancy have played a central role in the study of {0, 1}-valued CSPs, with deep connections to kernelization, query complexity, and the broader structural theory of VCSPs [BCK20, CJP20, LW20, Car22, BGJ +25, CCJK06, CCC +13, KTZ15, T ˇZ16, KKR17, CR ˇZ18]. We view extending these connections to valued CSPs through our real-valued analogs as a compelling direction for future research, continuing the rich line of structural work on CSPs. In the remainder of the introduction, we highlight some of the techniques that underlie our characterization theorems. 

1.3 Technical Overview 

1.3.1 Building Stronger Graph Sparsifiers Setting Up the Basics To help build intuition and understanding of the exact implications of Theorem 1.1, we start by showing how this theorem can be used to easily derive the existence of near-linear size spectral graph sparsifiers. In order to do so, we recall the equivalent “code” formulation of the spectrum of a graph G = ( V, E ). Indeed, we define CG ⊆ RE

, which has one codeword for each vector x ∈ RV . For such a vector x, the corresponding codeword c(x) ∈ RE

x ∈ Ψi, and we set b(i)2 = ( xu − xv)2 > 0 for the single edge e ∈ Ei.2. (Complete Shattering Condition) For each i ∈ [p], if |Ei| ≥ 2, there exists b(i)1 , b (i)2 ∈ R,

b(i)1 < b (i)2 (1 − ϵ) such that for every B ⊆ Ei, there is an assignment x ∈ Ψi such that for

For a collection of edges E1, . . . E k which satisfies these conditions, the CVNRD is then defined to be P 

> i

Note that we are free to choose the parameters χ, ρ to our advantage (although within reason, as we pay polynomial factors in their inverse in the sparsifier size). Thus, we will seek to show: 13 Theorem 1.7. Let G = ( V, E ) be a graph on n vertices and m edges. Let ϵ > 0. Then, CVNRD

Note that if we show this theorem, then we immediately obtain (1 ± ϵ) spectral sparsifiers of size O(n · poly(log( n), ϵ −1)) via Theorem 1.1. 

Understanding the Witness Structure The key observation we make is that because of the complete shattering condition, each block of edges Ei cannot have any short cycles. In fact, we show that Ei cannot have any cycles of length ≤ log( n); by standard girth-density tradeoffs, this immediately implies that each block of edges Ei contains O(n) edges. To understand why there cannot be any short cycles in a block of edges Ei, we proceed by contradiction. So let us suppose that there is a cycle C ⊆ Ei with length ℓ ≤ log( n). And for now, let us suppose that ℓ is odd. Then, the complete shattering condition guarantees us that we can find an assignment x ∈ RV such that for every single edge e = ( u, v ) ∈ C, ( xu − xv)2 ∈ b(i)2 · [1 , 1 + ϵ · χ]. For simplicity, let us ignore this ϵ · χ error term; in the full version of the proof this error term is chosen to be sufficiently small so that it is immaterial for cycles of size ≤ log( n). Thus, this implies that there is an assignment x ∈ RV such that for every edge ( u, v ) in the cycle, |xu − xv| =

q

b(i)2 .We can write this as xu − xv = σu,v ·

q

b(i)2 , where σu,v ∈ {± 1}. Now, we order the edges in the cycle, and sum their energies: 

X

> e=( u,v )∈C

(xu − xv) = X

> e=( u,v )∈C

σu,v ·

q

b(i)2 ≥

q

b(i)2 .

In particular, this last inequality follows because there are an odd number of edges in the cycle, and so P 

> e=( u,v )∈C

σu,v cannot be 0; it must be either ≤ − 1 or ≥ 1. But this then reveals a contradiction, as by definition, if one sums over the edges of the cycle, it must be the case that P 

> e=( u,v )∈C

(xu − xv) = 0 (each vertex appears once in the positive direction, and once in the negative direction). It may seem that this above approach does not rule out cycles of even length. This is true in the sense that we cannot use the same type of shattering assignment to rule out these kinds of cycles. Instead, we pick a “distinguished” edge e∗ ∈ C, and consider an assignment x ∈ RV such that for every edge e = ( u, v ) ∈ C − e∗, ( xu − xv)2 = b(i)2 , and for the last edge e∗ = ( u∗, v ∗), (xu∗ − xv∗ )2 = b(i)1 . Repeating the above argument shows that P 

> e=( u,v )∈C−e∗

(xu − xv) ≥

q

b(i)2 .All that’s left is to observe that

(xu − xv) ≥ X

q

which again yields a contradiction. In this final inequality, we have used the fact that b(i)1 and b(i)2

are separated from one another by a factor of (1 − ϵ). Together, this shows that there can be no short cycles within each block of edges Ei.14 Understanding the Off-Block Structure The preceding paragraphs show that within each block Ei, there can be no short cycles. But, we also know that if the combined set E1 ∪ E2 ∪ · · · ∪ Ep

has ≥ 100 n edges (for instance), then there must be some cycle C of length ≤ log( n) in the union 

of these blocks of edges. Moreover, because no single block contains short cycles, it must be the case that C uses edges from at least two different blocks. WLOG, we will assume that these blocks are E1 and E2. Now, let us look at the edges in E1 ∩ C; as above, the full proof will condition on whether |E1 ∩ C| is odd or even, but for the brief exposition here, we consider when |E1 ∩ C|

is odd. Just as above, we then consider the assignment x ∈ RV (which is guaranteed to exist because of the complete shattering condition) which gives every edge e = ( u, v ) ∈ E1 ∩ C energy (xu − xv)2 = b(i)2 . Just as above, because there is an odd number of edges in E1 ∩ C, this means that P 

> e=( u,v )∈C∩E1

(xu − xv) ≥

q

b(1) 2 .

Now, for this same assignment x ∈ RV , we look at the “off-block” energy (i.e., the energy coming from edges in E̸=1 ). As above, we know that in any cycle C, P 

> e=( u,v )∈C

(xu − xv) = 0; because the length of the cycle is ≤ log( n), this means there must be some edge ( u, v ) ∈ C \ E1 such that |xu − xv| ≥

. This immediately gives a contradiction though; indeed, the energy on this edge is then ≥ b(1) 2 

> log 2(n)

b(1) 2 .

This then concludes Theorem 1.7; if there are too many edges in the CVNRD witness, this implies there is a short cycle in the graph, which in turn must use edges from multiple different blocks. By choosing a careful shattering assignment from one of these blocks, this forces some edge in the rest of the cycle to pick up too much energy, thereby violating the off-block condition. 

Generalizing to Higher Powers and Hypergraphs Importantly, nothing in the above argu-ment relies on the specific choice of energy being ( xu − xv)2; in general, we may consider the energy on an edge to be |xu − xv|t for some t ≥ 1. The same argument will work for showing that in each individual block of edges Ei that there are no short cycles. Thus, when we consider a witness to CVNRD with (say) ≥ 100 n many edges, we can invoke the same argument above to show that there are cycles of length < log( n) which thereby must use edges from multiple different blocks (say that E1 is one such block). As above, we then repeat the argument to show that, by exploiting the complete shattering condition, we can create a potential difference between all of the non-E1 edges. Note that this will now look like P 

> e=( u,v )∈C∩E1

(xu − xv) ≥ ϵ ·



b(1) 2

1/t 

. Importantly, this means there must be some edge in E̸=1 which receives potential difference ≥ ϵ· 

> 
> b(1) 2
> 1/t
> log( n)

, and thus receives energy ≥ ϵt·b(1) 2 

> log t(n)

. By choosing ρ to now depend on ϵt, this then violates the maximum entrywise off-block energy condition. The generalization to hypergraphs is more subtle, so we only very briefly give the intuition. The key insight turns out to be that any hypergraph which satisfies the conditions of Definition 1.5 can be turned into a graph satisfying the same conditions, while only paying a log 2(n) factor in the size of the witness. This immediately bounds the CVNRD of a hypergraph’s energy by O(n log 2(n)); indeed, if the size of a witness is any larger, this yields a CVNRD witness in a graph of size ω(n), which then violates the bounds provided above. 15 Proving this simulation statement (i.e., that any hypergraph is effectively simulating a graph) relies on an invocation of the so-called “graph-theoretic Sauer-Shelah Lemma” due to Cesa-Bianchi and Haussler [CBH98]. For more context, the complete shattering condition in a hypergraph says that for a block of hyperedges Ei, there exists b(i)1 , b (i)2 ∈ R, b(i)1 < b (i)2 (1 − ϵ) such that for every 

B ⊆ Ei, there is an assignment x ∈ Ψi such that for e ∈ B, max u,v ∈e(xu −xv)2 ∈ b(i)1 ·[1 , 1+ ϵ·χ] and for e ∈ Ei −B, max u,v (xu −xv)2 ∈ b(i)2 ·[1 , 1+ ϵ·χ]. Whenever max u,v (xu −xv)2 ∈ b(i)2 ·[1 , 1+ ϵ·χ], there must be a “representative pair” of vertices u, v ∈ e which are contributing the energy ( xu − xv)2 ≈

b(i)2 . For each hyperedge, and each assignment x ∈ RV , we thus associate a label ∈  n

> 2

 corresponding to which “representative pair” witnesses this high energy. The graph-theoretic Sauer-Shelah Lemma [CBH98] then shows that one can find a slightly smaller subset of hyperedges E′ 

> i

⊆ Ei such that, whenever a hyperedge e receives energy max u,v ∈e(xu − xv)2 ≈ b(i)2 , it is always the same pair (u, v )which is the “representative pair”. This is then formally translated into a graph sparsification problem, where each hyperedge is replaced with its representative pair. 

1.3.2 Building NRD-Size Sparsifiers for {0, 1}-Valued Codes Without Gilmer Background The preceding discussion gives intuition about how to use CVNRD to understand the sparsifiability of real-valued structures. In this section, we now give intuition about how we actually prove our sparsifiability results. To do so, we start by re-deriving a result of Brakensiek and Guruswami [BG25] which characterizes the sparsifiability of {0, 1}-valued codes by their NRD, but importantly, this proof does not use Gilmer’s entropy method [Gil22]. Recall that, given a code C ⊆ { 0, 1}m, the non-redundancy of C, denoted NRD( C), is the largest collection of indices 

a1, . . . a k ∈ [m] along with codewords c(1) , . . . c (k) such that for i ∈ [k] c(i) 

> ai

= 1, and c(i) 

> a̸=i

= 0. Equivalently, if one lines up the codewords in C as columns of an m × | C| matrix, NRD( C) is the largest identity submatrix (up to permutation of rows and columns). The key result which drives sparsification in [BG25] is essentially the following (which we have edited for clarity): 

Theorem 1.8 ([BG25] Decomposition Theorem) . Let C ⊆ { 0, 1}m consist of only codewords of Hamming weight ∈ [d/ 2, d ]. Then, for any ϵ > 0, there exists a set S ⊆ [m] of size |S| ≤ O(NRD (C)·

log 4(m)/ϵ 2) such that 

|C| ¯S | ≤ 2ϵ2·d/ 10000 .

In words, this theorem is stating that, for any code C with codewords of weight ∈ [d/ 2, d ], there is a small set of coordinates S (whose size scales only with the non-redundancy of C), such that, after peeling off the set S, the number of codewords in C drastically decreases. In particular, after peeling off this set of coordinates S, one can then randomly sample the remaining coordinates in ¯S at rate 1 /2 to get a set T ⊆ [m] − S with the guarantee that, with high probability over the random sampling, for every codeword c ∈ C,

X

> i∈S

ci + 2 · X

> i∈T

ci ∈ (1 ± ϵ) · X

> i∈[m]

ci.

Thus, this effectively reduces the number of coordinates in the code by a factor of 2, and repeated application is used to ultimately produce sparsifiers of size eO(NRD( C)/ϵ 2). The proof of this decomposition theorem in [BG25] relies on an intricate analysis using Gilmer’s entropy method [Gil22], the Sauer-Shelah lemma, along with an exploitation of the duality between so-called θ-covers and θ-sparse distributions over a code C. In what follows, we take an entirely 16 different approach and show that the only essential ingredient from this list is the Sauer-Shelah lemma. Towards this end, we recall this lemma: 

Lemma 1.9 (Sauer-Shelah Lemma, [Sau72] [She72].) . Let C ⊆ { 0, 1}m. Then, there exists a set 

A ⊆ [m], |A| ≥ log( |C|)log( m+1) such that C|A = {0, 1}A.

Intuitively, this shows that whenever a code C has a lot of codewords, it must be the case that there is some “complete subcode” hiding inside the code. This is denoted by {0, 1}A: meaning that we have all possible patterns of 0’s and 1’s on those coordinates. Importantly, the size of A scales with the number of codewords in C.

Our Approach to Proving Theorem 1.8 Our approach to proving Theorem 1.8 is direct; we let C, d, ϵ be given as in the statement of their theorem, and we let S be the smallest possible set 

such that |C ¯S | ≤ 2ϵ2d/ 10000 . Our goal is then to show that if S is large, then there must be a similarly large witness to NRD inside the code C.As a first observation, we can see that if S is large, then there is a large set of “disjoint complete subcodes” that we can find inside the code C. Indeed, let us consider an iterative algorithm which peels off complete subcodes. To start, we initialize a set R = ∅ ⊆ [m]. Naturally then, it should be the case that |R| < |S|. Because S is the smallest possible set such that |C ¯S | ≤ 2ϵ2d/ 10000 , it will also be the case that |C ¯R| > 2ϵ2d/ 10000 . Now, we can invoke Lemma 1.9. This shows that there is some subset A1 ⊆ [m] − R of size |A1| ≥ log( |C ¯R|)log( m+1) ≈ ϵ2d 

> log( m)

(for the convenience of the reader, we will avoid carrying around constant factors), such that C|A1 = {0, 1}A1 .After we find such a subcode, we will now say that these coordinates A1 are effectively “used up”, and so we add them to the set R. Now, assuming it is still true that |R| < |S|, we again repeat the observation that |C ¯R| > 2ϵ2d/ 10000 and peel off another set A2, |A2| ≈ ϵ2d 

> log( m)

such that 

C|A2 = {0, 1}A2 . Moreover, it must be the case that A2 is completely disjoint from A1, as we had already added A1 to R at this point. Note that we can keep repeating this procedure until 

|R| ≥ | S|. At this point, we will have recovered sets A1, . . . A k such that P 

> i∈[k]

|Ai| ≥ | S|, for each 

Ai, C|Ai = {0, 1}Ai , and |Ai| ≈ ϵ2d 

> log( m)

. For convenience, we will let C1 ⊆ C denote a minimal set of codewords which witnesses ( C1)|A1 = {0, 1}A1 , and similarly denote Ci as the collection of codewords for Ai. Although the resulting collection of codewords is not block-diagonal, we do know that, because every codeword in C had weight ≤ d, for each codeword c ∈ Ci, wt( c|A̸ =i ) ≤ d.The result of this sequence of operations is a collection of subcodes and sets A1, . . . A k which has the form of Fig. 1. To prove Theorem 1.8, our goal simply to show that this collection of subcodes 

contains a large identity matrix somewhere! Our general guiding principle is that we will continue to process this collection of subcodes until it becomes truly block-diagonal.

1.3.3 Going Beyond {0, 1}-Valued Codes to Codes with Bounded Aspect Ratio 

There is one important manner in which the proof strategy above is actually stronger than that of Brakensiek and Guruswami [BG25]. Namely, rather than simply returning an identity matrix, the actual witness that is uncovered is of the form of Fig. 6, where each block is a complete sub-code of large size (proportional to the parameter d). This turns out to be the key strengthening that is required when dealing with codes beyond {0, 1}-values. To illustrate this, let us consider a code C ⊆ { 0, 1, 2}m, and let us suppose that every codeword 

c ∈ C has Hamming weight in the range [ d, 2d] for some integer d. It is not hard to show that, just as Theorem 1.8 suffices for building sparsifiers in the {0, 1}-valued setting, the key for building sparsifiers in the {0, 1, 2}-valued setting is to consider the smallest set S ⊆ [m] such that |C| ¯S | ≤ 

2ϵ2d/ 10000 .From such a starting point, we can now repeat the procedure outlined in Section 1.3.2: we initialize a set R = ∅ ⊆ [m]. Because S is the smallest possible set such that |C ¯S | ≤ 2ϵ2d/ 10000 , it will also be the case that |C ¯R| > 2ϵ2d/ 10000 . In Section 1.3.2, we then invoke Lemma 1.9, which shows that there is some subset A1 ⊆ [m]−R of large size such that C|A1 = {0, 1}A1 . Unfortunately, 22 this is a statement specialized to {0, 1}-valued codes. In this more general setting, the appropriate analog turns out to be the generalization of Sauer-Shelah to Natarajan dimension :

Lemma 1.10 (Sauer-Shelah Lemma for Discrete Alphabets [SSBD14].) . Let C ⊆ { 0, 1, 2}m. Then, there exists a set A ⊆ [m], |A| = Ω 

 log( |C|)log( m)



along with two symbols a̸ = b ∈ { 0, 1, 2} such that 

{a, b }A ⊆ C|A.

With this in hand, we can pick up where we were; Lemma 1.10 shows that there is some subset 

A1 ⊆ [m] − R of size Ω 

 ϵ2d

To recap, this means that we can peel off a set of eO(BACNRD( C)/ϵ 2) many coordinates from our starting code C such that, after peeling, we can afford to sample at rate 1 /2 while still producing a sparsifier. Carefully repeating this procedure then yields sparsifiers of total size 

eO(BACNRD( C)/ϵ 2). This effectively yields the characterization of Theorem 1.4, at least in the case where the input code has discretely separated values. 23 1.3.4 Going to Continuous Values With Bounded Aspect Ratio 

Generalizing to continuous codes is more subtle than simply the discrete alphabet setting as we are no longer guaranteed that there is any choice of a set S such that |C ¯S | ≤ 2ϵ2d/ 10000 . Indeed, in the continuous setting, the number of codewords can even be infinite. It turns out that rather than studying the number of codewords in the code, the right notion to work with is the size of the smallest ℓ∞-cover of a subcode. To understand these notions, for the rest of this section we focus on codes C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , 2]) m.

Definition 1.8. Let C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , 2]) m and ϵ > 0. An ϵ-cover is a set C′ ⊆ C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , 2]) m

such that for every c ∈ C there exists c′ ∈ C′ such that, for all i ∈ [m], 

ci ∈ [c′ 

> i

± ϵ]Let |Cover( C, ϵ )| denote the size of the smallest such cover. 5

Thus, the appropriate analog of the smallest set S which reduces the number of codewords in C

is to peel the smallest set which reduces the size of the smallest cover on the resulting code; i.e., the smallest set S such that |Cover( C| ¯S , ϵ )| ≤ 2ϵ2d/ 10000 . Just as before, where having a small number of codewords suffices for random sampling to build sparsifiers, having a small cover also turns out to be sufficient. However, just as above, we then also need an analog of Sauer-Shelah in this setting. If it is the case that a code C satisfies |Cover( C| ¯S , ϵ )| ≥ 2ϵ2d/ 10000 , can we find a large, complete subcode? The answer again turns out to be yes, using a result of Alon, Ben-David, Cesa-Bianchi, and Haussler [ABCH97] which relates cover sizes to fat-shattering dimension: 

Definition 1.9. For a code C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , 2]) m, the fat-shattering dimension of C with parameter 

ϵ > 0, denoted by fdim( C, ϵ ) is the largest ℓ ≤ m such that there exists A ⊆ [m], |A| = ℓ along with a vector γ ∈ [0 , 2] A, such that for every B ⊆ A, there is a vector v ∈ C such that for i ∈ B,

vi ≥ γi + ϵ, and for i ∈ A − B, vi ≤ γi − ϵ.With this definition, we can then introduce the appropriate analog of Sauer-Shelah: 

Lemma 1.11 (Lemma 3.5 of [ABCH97]) . Let C ⊆ [0 , 2] m be a code, let ϵ > 0, and let d =fdim( C, ϵ/ 4) . Then, 

|Cover( C, ϵ )| ≤ 2 ·

 16 mϵ2

d log(4 em/dϵ )

.

In particular, when |Cover( C, ϵ )| is large, this immediately implies that fdim( C, ϵ/ 4) is large as well! Plugging this into the same framework as discussed in Section 1.3.2 then essentially leads to the statement of Theorem 1.4. 

1.3.5 Going to Unbounded Aspect Ratios 

Going from codes C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , 2]) m to C ⊆ Rm 

> ≥0

adds several more layers of subtlety to designing sparsifiers. This is reflected by the more careful definition of CVNRD, as Definition 1.5 already deviates from the definition we used in Section 1.3.4. Definition 1.5 does not keep the “perfectly block diagonal” structure that we saw in the {0, 1}-setting and the bounded aspect ratio setting, and this is inherent: E.g., if one takes the resulting structure from Fig. 7 and replaces every 0 with       

> 5When Cis {0}(or empty), we set |Cover( C, ϵ )|= 0.

24 a small value, say 1 /m 2, then the sparsifiability of the code is entirely unchanged. However, there is now no way to ensure that the off-diagonal entries are in fact identically 0. The reasons for this modification are several-fold, as it turns out that characterizing real-valued sparsifiability with unbounded aspect ratios is significantly more intricate than the bounded aspect ratio setting. We summarize some of the key barriers below: 1. The first key subtlety is that when we try to follow the same path laid out in Section 1.3.4, our goal is to have a statement which says that if |Cover( C| ¯R, ϵ )| ≥ 2ϵ2d/ 10000 , then it must be the case that there is some large, “complete” shattering code which is contained inside of C.Unfortunately, such a statement is already not correct. The true formulation of Lemma 1.11 has a dependence in the exponent which looks like d log(2 em · cmax  

> cmin

), where cmax is the maxi-mum weight symbol which ever appears in a codeword, and cmin is the smallest weight symbol which appears in a codeword. In this setting with unbounded aspect ratios, this ratio cmax 

> cmin

can already be too large, thereby worsening the resulting lower bounds that we get for d (and thus, the size of our shattering witnesses). 2. Even worse, it is no longer clear that our target bound on the cover sizes is even useful. Indeed, suppose we had a guarantee that |Cover( C| ¯R, ϵ )| < 2ϵ2d/ 10000 . Our goal would now be to argue that random sampling at rate 1 /2 will preserve all codeword weights. But, this argument crucially relies on Chernoff-like concentration. Just as above, the concentration probabilities one gets from Chernoff also have a dependence on the aspect ratio cmax  

> cmin

. This effect is so strong that if cmax  

> cmin

≈ m, then we even lose the guarantee that a single codeword 

has its weight preserved with high probability, much less that all codewords have their weight preserved. 3. Not only this, but in the discussion in Section 1.3.2 and Section 1.3.4, we assumed that we were given a code C where every codeword had approximately the same Hamming weight. Implicitly, we were using the fact that if codewords have the same Hamming weight, then they also (at least approximately) have the same actual weight . Such a statement is no longer true in the Rm 

> ≥0

setting! In our ultimate proof, we thus proceed by carefully analyzing a sequence of carefully defined auxiliary codes which now do have bounded aspect ratios. Roughly speaking, we focus on symbols in some range [ a, a (1 + ϵ)], with the goal of saying that, for every codeword which has a significant portion of its weight coming from symbols in this range, the contribution coming from these symbols is preserved to a (1 ± ϵ) factor when we do random sampling. This forms the foundation for producing our CVNRD sparsifiers, and alleviates the aforementioned issues: bounded aspect ratios remove the extra factors in Lemma 1.11, they improve the concentration when invoking Chernoff, and they allow for a simpler relationship between the Hamming weight of a codeword and its actual weight. Unfortunately, this approach raises other issues: it is true that we can now prove relationships between the sparsifiability of our original code and the CVNRD of these auxiliary codes . But can we guarantee that these CVNRD witnesses from the auxiliary codes can be translated into CVNRD witnesses of our original code ? By again relying on the careful definition of our auxiliary codes, it turns out that the answer is yes. This proof uses a more careful invocation of the off-diagonal weight reduction technique used in Section 1.3.2 to reduce the weight of each codeword in its off-diagonal coordinates as much as possible, along with careful codeword weight counting which shows that if the CVNRD witnesses from the auxiliary codes are ever too small , then they can be effectively ignored as barriers to sparsification. 25 There are still several more obstacles dealing with e.g., the “tightness” of the grouping of the co-ordinates in Definition 1.5 (see the χ-parameter in the “Complete Block Shattering” condition), and how to formally prove that our sampling scheme produces sparsifiers, but we invite the interested reader to Section 6 for the complete description.

Remark 2.1. Note that throughout this work, when we deal with codes C ⊆ Rm

> ≥0

For a code C, we define its unweighted sparsifiability as: 

Definition 2.2. For C ⊆ Rm 

> ≥0

|Supp( C≤d)| ≤ d · NRD (C).

2.3 VC Dimension, Sauer-Shelah, and Discrete Relatives 

We start by recapping several useful notions that we will require. The first is that of VC-dimension: 

Definition 2.7. Let C ⊆ { 0, 1}m be an arbitrary code. The VC-dimension of C (denoted by VCDim( C) is the largest integer ℓ such that there exists A ⊆ [m] such that |A| = ℓ and {0, 1}A ⊆

C|A.The works of [Sau72, She72] showed the following theorem relating the size of a code with its VC-dimension: 

Theorem 2.4 (Sauer-Shelah [Sau72], [She72]) . Let C ⊆ { 0, 1}m be an arbitrary code. Then, 

|C| ≤ (m + 1) VCDim( C).

We will also frequently make use of an extension of VC-dimension to alphabets of size larger than 2, using so-called Natarajan dimension: 

Definition 2.8. For a code C ⊆ { a1, a 2, . . . a k}m, we say that the Natarajan dimension of C,denoted by NDim( C) is the largest size of a set T ⊆ [m] such that: 27 1. There exists f0, f 1 : T → { a1, a 2, . . . a k} such that ∀i ∈ T : f0(i)̸ = f1(i). 2. Q

|C| ≤ (mk 2)NDim( C).

2.4 Continuous Notions of VC-Dimension 

Because we eventually work with sparsifying continuous codes C ⊆ [0 , 1] m, we also need continuous analogs of VC-dimension. For this, we use of the notion of fat-shattering dimension and cover sizes: 

Definition 2.9. For a code C ⊆ [1 , k ]m, the fat-shattering dimension of C with parameter ϵ > 0, denoted by fdim( C, ϵ ) is the largest ℓ ≤ m such that there exists A ⊆ [m], |A| = ℓ along with a vector γ ∈ [1 , k ]A, such that for every B ⊆ A, there is a vector v ∈ C such that for i ∈ B, vi ≥ γi +ϵ,and for i ∈ A − B, vi ≤ γi − ϵ.

Definition 2.10. Let C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m be a code. For a parameter ϵ > 0, we say that C′ ⊆

({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m is an ϵ-cover of C if, for every c ∈ C there exists a c′ ∈ C′ such that for every i ∈ [m], 

ci ∈ [c′ 

> i

· (1 ± ϵ)] .

We use |Cover( C, ϵ )| to denote the size of the smallest ϵ-cover of C. When C contains only the all-zeros codeword (or no codewords), for simplicity we say the size of the smallest cover is 0. The reason that we will be interested in covers is that sparsifying a cover suffices for sparsifying the original code: 

Claim 2.8. Let C ⊆ [0 , 1] m be a code, let ϵ′ > 0, and let C′ be an ϵ′/4-cover of C. Then, if a set of coordinates S ⊆ [m] along with weights ˆwS is a (1 ± ϵ′/4) -sparsifier of C′, then S, ˆwS is also a 

(1 ± ϵ′) sparsifier of C.Proof. Indeed, consider any codeword c ∈ C, and let c′ denote the corresponding covering codeword in C′. Then, we know that for every i ∈ [m], ci ∈ (1 ± ϵ′/4) · c′

> i

. Thus, it must be the case that wt( c) ∈ (1 ± ϵ′/4)wt( c′), and that fwt( c|S ) ∈ (1 ± ϵ′/4) fwt( c′|S ). All together then, we see that 

fwt( c|S ) ∈ (1 ± ϵ′/4) · fwt( c′|S ) ∈ (1 ± ϵ′/4) 2 · wt( c′) ∈ (1 ± ϵ′/4) 3 · wt( c) ∈ (1 ± ϵ′)wt( c),

as we desire. 29 We will also frequently use the following “submodularity” property of cover sizes: 

Claim 2.9. Let C = C(1) ∪ C(2) ∪ · · · ∪ C(p), and let ϵ > 0. Then,

# 3 Warming-Up: Characterizing {0, 1}m-Sparsifiability With Sauer-Shelah 

In this section, we show how to (essentially) re-derive Theorem 4.16 of [BG25] using a purely combinatorial argument (notably, without using Gilmer’s entropy method). Instead, the only non-trivial tool we use is Theorem 2.4. To this end, we first re-state Theorem 4.16 of [BG25]: 6

Theorem 3.1 (Theorem 4.16 of [BG25]) . For any C ⊆ { 0, 1}m, integer d ≥ 1, and real λ ≥ 1,there exists I ⊆ [m] of size at most 400 λ·NRD (C)log(2 m) such that (C≤d)¯I has at most m · 2100 d log 4(2 m)/λ 

many codewords. 

3.1 Finding “Dense” Subcodes 

To prove this, we define S ⊆ [m] to be the smallest set of coordinates such that ( C≤d) ¯S has at most 

m · 2100 d log 4(2 m)/λ many codewords. Our goal will be to show that if S is large then we can in fact recover a witness to NRD which is proportionally large. As our starting point, we make the following observation: 

Claim 3.2. For any C ⊆ { 0, 1}m, integer d ≥ 1, and real λ ≥ 1, let S ⊆ [m] to be the smallest set of coordinates such that (C≤d) ¯S has at most m · 2100 d log 4(2 m)/λ many codewords. Then, there exist disjoint A1, . . . A ℓ ⊆ [m] along with C1, . . . C ℓ ⊆ C≤d such that: 

> 6Note that we are not concerned with exactly achieving the same logarithmic factors - the restatement below has added a few such factors.

≥ | S|.Proof. Let S be defined as above. In particular, this means that for any set R ⊆ [m] such that

(WLOG we can assume equality here by truncating the set Ai if it is larger). We let Ci ⊆ C≤d denote a minimal set of codewords such that ( Ci)|Ai = {0, 1}Ai , and then set 

Ri+1 = Ri ∪ Ai (intuitively, Ri+1 is the set of coordinates which have already peeled by the i + 1st iteration). At the termination of this procedure, we have recovered a sequence of disjoint A1, . . . A ℓ ⊆ [m]along with C1, . . . C ℓ ⊆ C≤d such that: 1. |Ai| = 100 d log 3(2 m)

≥ | S|.Note that by the minimality of Ci, it will also be the case that |Ci| = 2 |Ai|. I.e., that there is exactly one codeword in Ci for each pattern of 0’s and 1’s in Ai.Recall that ultimately, our goal is to bound the size of S in terms of the non-redundancy of C. To do this, we will show that we can find a large NRD witness inside this collection A1, . . . A ℓ, C 1, . . . C ℓ.In the following sections, we prove this in a sequence of abstract steps, and then show how we can use this to conclude the theorem.

34 3.4 Making the Subcodes Block-Diagonal

# 4 Characterizing Sparsifiability in Continuous Codes with Bounded Aspect Ratio 

In this section, we prove our sparsification theorem for unweighted continuous -valued codes with bounded aspect ratio, and show its rough almost-equivalence to the real-valued non-redundancy. We will focus on codes C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m. Recall that here we define the non-redundancy as follows: 

Definition 4.1. Let C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m. The bounded-aspect continuous non-redundancy of C

with parameter ϵ, denoted by BACNRD( C, ϵ ), is the maximum integer ℓ such that there exists 

A1, . . . A p ⊆ [m] and C1, . . . C p ⊆ C, with Ppi=1 |Ai| = ℓ, and: 1. For every i ∈ [p], if |Ai| = 1, then ( Ci)|Ai̸ = {0}.2. For every i ∈ [p] if |Ai| ≥ 2, then there is a choice of vector γ ∈ [1 , k ]Ai , such that for every 

B ⊆ Ai, there is a codeword c ∈ Ci such that for b ∈ B, vb ≥ γb + ϵ, and for b ∈ Ai − B,

vb ≤ γb − ϵ.3. For every j̸ = i, ( Ci)|Aj = 0 Aj .For this definition, we have the following characterization: 

Theorem 4.1. Let C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m with k = O(1) . Then, 1. There exists ϵ′ such that ϵ′ = Ω( ϵ) and US( C, ϵ ′) = Ω( ϵ · BACNRD (C, ϵ )) .2. For ϵ′ = ϵ/ 256 log( m), US( C, ϵ ) = eO(BACNRD (C, ϵ ′)/ϵ 4).

40 4.1 Basic Properties of BACNRD 

Remark 4.2. Immediately, we can observe that BACNRD( C) ≥ NRD( ˆC), as any witness to a NRD lower bound in ˆC is also a valid witness to BACNRD. We also have the following convenient inheritance property: 

Remark 4.3. Let C ⊆ Rm be a code. Let S ⊆ [m], and let C′ ⊆ C. Then BACNRD( C′|S ) ≤

BACNRD( C). Essentially, the non-redundancy of the parent instance is always at least as that of any sub-instance. 

4.2 Lower Bound 

To start, we prove the lower bound of Theorem 4.1.

4.3 Upper Bound 

4.3.1 Constructing the Sparsifier 

We first present the algorithm constructing the sparsifier and show that this indeed returns a (1 ±ϵ)sparsifier of our starting code. Then, in the subsequent sections, we will prove an upper bound on the size of this sparsifier in terms of the BACNRD. For a code C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m, we let ˆC denote the {0, 1}-version of the code, where every non-zero symbol gets mapped to 1. To start, we let d range over {1, 2, 4, 8, . . . m }. Now, for each choice of d, we consider the code ˆC[d/ 2,d ]. Because this is a code over {0, 1}m, we can then directly invoke Theorem 2.2 to conclude that for any λ, there is a set Id of size ≤ 2λNRD( C) log(4 m) such that ˆC[d/ 2,d ]|¯I has ≤ m · 23d log 2(2 m)/λ many codewords. For our use case, we set λ = 10000 · log 4(m)k2/ϵ ′2, for ϵ′ = ϵ/ 16 log( m), where ϵ is our ultimate desired accuracy for our sparsifier. Note that when d is very small, d ≤ 10000 k2 log 3(m)/ϵ ′2, we invoke Claim 2.3 to in fact remove the entire support of ˆC[d/ 2,d ] (i.e., Id = ˆC[d/ 2,d ]). In this way, we can ensure that we do not ever have to worry about preserving the smallest weight codewords, as their supports are explicitly taken care of. For the other choices of d, after removing I1, . . . I m from ˆC, our code then has sufficiently few codewords such that sampling the coordinates at rate 1 /2 would yield a (1 ± ϵ′) sparsifier of ˆC.The only remaining roadblock is that our goal is to sparsify C, not just ˆC.To this end, we also consider the codes C[d/ 2,d ] = {Class(ˆ c) : ˆ c ∈ ˆC[d/ 2,d ]}, where these codes are now defined over ( {0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m. For our final piece of notation, we let Sd ⊂ [m] denote the 

smallest set of coordinates such that

4.3.2 Bounding the Size of Peeled Sets by Upper-Triangular Sub-Codes 

In this section, we prove the following key lemma: 

Lemma 4.7. Let C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m be a code, let ϵ′ > 0, let d ≥ 10000 k2 log 3(m)/ϵ ′2, and let Ld

denote the size of the smallest set T ⊆ [m] such that 

Cover( C| ¯T , ϵ ′/4) ≤ 2ϵ′2d/ 10000 k2 log( m).

Further, suppose that every codeword c ∈ ˆC satisfies wt( c) ≤ d and that | ˆC| ≤ m·23d log 2(2 m)ϵ′2/10000 k2 log 4(m).Then, one can find sets of coordinates A1, . . . A p, and sets of codewords C1, . . . C p such that: 1. For every i ∈ [p], there exists a γ ∈ [1 , k ]Ai such that, for every B ⊆ Ai there exists a codeword 

c ∈ Ci such that, for b ∈ B, cb ≥ γb + ϵ′/16 and for b ∈ Ai − B, cb ≤ γb − ϵ′/16 .2. For i < j ∈ [p], Ci|Aj = 0 .3. Each |Ai| = Ω( ϵ′2d) and Ppi=1 |Ai| = eΩ( ϵ′2 · Ld).4. For i ∈ [p], there exists ˆc ∈ ˆC such that Ci ⊆ Class(ˆ c).

Intuitively, this lemma states that whenever we have to remove a lot of coordinates (rows) from our code in order to decrease the size of the cover, this is because there is a large upper-triangular matrix of a specific form contained in our code. Ultimately, we will show that we can even further modify this upper-triangular matrix to get it in our desired block-diagonal form. Now, to prove this lemma, we introduce an algorithm for producing a set of coordinates and codes of the desired form: 45 Algorithm 4: ConstructUpperTriangularWitness( C, d, L d, ϵ ′)

fdim( W,ϵ ′/16) log(8 emk/ fdim( W,ϵ ′/16) ϵ′)

≥ 2ϵ′2d/ 20000 k2 log( m).

Taking the logs of both sides yields that fdim( W, ϵ ′/16) log(8 emk/ fdim( W, ϵ ′/16) ϵ′) · log(64 mk 2/ϵ ′2) + 1 ≥ ϵ′2d/ 20000 k2 log( m).

Thus, we have that fdim( W, ϵ ′/16) ≥ ϵ′2d/ 20000 k2 log( m) − 1log(8 emk/ fdim( W, ϵ ′/16) ϵ′) · log(64 mk 2/ϵ ′2) = eΩ( ϵ′2d),

where we are using that k = O(1), that fdim( W, ϵ ′/16) ≤ m, and that ϵ′2d/ 20000 k2 log( m) = Ω(log( m)), hence the −1 in the numerator is negligible. In total, this means that for the code W = Class(ˆ ci)|[m]−Rd , the fat-shattering dimension with parameter ϵ′/16 is at least eΩ( ϵ′2d), which, as per Definition 2.9, exactly implies that we can find a set Yi ⊆ [m] − Rd, and γ ∈ [1 , k ]Yi such that for every B ⊆ Yi, there is a v ∈ Class(ˆ ci)|[m]−Rd such that for b ∈ B, vb ≥ γb + ϵ′/16 and for b ∈ Yi − B, vb ≤ γb − ϵ′/16. Letting Zi denote these vectors which realize the fat-shattering dimension then yields the claim. Now, we lower bound the size of Qd that is returned by our algorithm.

Observe that in iteration i, it is always the case that Yi ⊆ [m] − Rd, where Rd is the current set of “banned rows”. Now, consider the iteration b in which Zb is recovered as a set of codewords. At the end of this iteration, Rd ← Rd ∪ Supp(ˆ cb), where ˆ cb is the codeword in ˆC such that Zb ⊆ Class(ˆ cb)[m]−Rd . In particular, this ensures that for all future iterations a > b , it is the case that Supp(ˆ cb) = Supp( Zb) ⊆ Rd (these rows will never be recovered in the future), and thus, there is no coordinate in Ya for which ( Zb)a̸ = 0. 47 Now, we are ready to conclude our proof of Lemma 4.7: 

Proof of Lemma 4.7. Given a code of the form guaranteed by Lemma 4.7, we run Algorithm 4. By Claim 4.8 and Claim 4.9, we know that we can always run this algorithm to completion (i.e., that every line with an existential argument is valid). Now, we claim that the returned sets Qd = ( Y1, . . . Y i) and Cd = ( Z1, . . . Z i) satisfy our desired conditions in Lemma 4.7. For clarity, we let i = p, and let Y1, . . . Y p = A1, . . . Lastly, we show that for a ∈ [p], there exists ˆ c ∈ ˆC such that Ci ⊆ Class(ˆ c). This again follows by construction (see Algorithm 4). This concludes the proof. While this lemma already suffices for establishing the existence of a large upper-triangular matrix whenever Ld is large, our ultimate goal is to show the existence of a large block diagonal matrix . # 5 Sparsifying Discrete Codes with Bounded Aspect Ratio 

In this section, we strengthen the previous results for the specific setting of sparsifying codes 

C ⊆ { 0, a 1, . . . a k}m, where each ai ≥ 1, ai = O(1), and |ai − aj | = Ω(1). For this setting, we have the following notion of discrete-valued non-redundancy: 

Definition 5.1. Let C ⊆ { 0, a 1, . . . a k}m. The bounded aspect ratio, discrete-valued non-redundancy of C, denoted by BADNRD( C) is the maximum integer ℓ such that there exists A1, . . . A p ⊆ [m]and C1, . . . C p ⊆ C, with Ppi=1 |Ai| = ℓ, and: 1. For every i ∈ [p], if |Ai| = 1, then ( Ci)|Ai ∈ { a1, . . . a k}.2. For every i ∈ [p], if |Ai| ≥ 2, then ( Ci)|Ai = {b0, b 1}|Ai| for some {b0, b 1} ⊆ { 0, a 1, . . . a k}.3. For every j̸ = i, ( Ci)|Aj = 0 Aj .In this section, we show the following theorem characterizing unweighted sparsifiability of discrete-valued codes in terms of their BADNRD: 

Theorem 5.1. Let C ⊆ { 0, a 1, a 2, . . . a k}m such that for each i ∈ [k], a i ≥ 1, ai = O(1) , and for all i̸ = j ∈ [k], |ai − aj | = Ω(1) . Then: 1. There is a choice of ϵ = Ω(1) such that US( C, ϵ ) = Ω( BADNRD (C)) .2. US( C, ϵ ) = eO(BADNRD (C)/ϵ 4).

Note that this assumption that ai ≥ 1 is without loss of generality, as if any ai ≤ 1, we can simply re-scale all of the ai’s without altering sparsifiability. 

5.1 Discrete-Valued Non-Redundancy Characterizations 

In this section, we prove both parts of Theorem 5.1. 

5.1.1 Proof of Lower Bound 

The lower bound follows from showing that any BADNRD( C) witness also constitutes an BACNRD( C, ϵ )witness, for some ϵ = Ω(1). 52 Proof of Theorem 5.1, Item 1. Indeed, let the code C be given as in the theorem statement, and let C1, . . . C p, A 1, . . . A p denote the maximum size BADNRD witness (using Definition 5.1). Now, let ϵ = min i̸=j∈[k] |ai − aj |. By definition then, we know that C1, . . . C p, A 1, . . . A p constitutes an BACNRD( C, ϵ/ 2) witness, (now in the notion of Definition 4.1), as in each subcode Ci, we know that Ci|Ai = {b0, b 1}Ai , and thus we can use the vector γ = (( b0 + b1)/2) Ai . In particular, this means that BACNRD( C, ϵ/ 2) ≥ BADNRD( C). We can then invoke Theorem 4.1, Item 1, to conclude that there exists an ϵ′ = Ω( ϵ) = Ω(1) such that the ϵ′ unweighted sparsifiability of C is Ω( ϵ · BACNRD( C, ϵ/ 2)) = Ω(BADNRD( C)). Note that it is possible that (( b0 + b1)/2) ∈ [1 /2, 1], and thus may not exactly fit into the framework of BACNRD; in this case, one may simply double all of the entries of the code C, and invoke the BACNRD lower bound. 

5.1.2 Proof of Upper Bound 

As above, the upper bound follows from observing that each complete sub-code (in the notion of Definition 4.1) must still contain a large witness to the BADNRD. We present this argument below. 

Proof of Theorem 5.1, Item 2. Indeed, let ϵ be given. Then, for any subset of the coordinates of C

(which we will still refer to as C), and for ϵ′ = ϵ/ 256 log( m), we can sparsify C to eO(BACNRD( C, ϵ ′)/ϵ 4)many coordinates. To prove the theorem at hand, we claim that BACNRD( C, ϵ ′) = eO(BADNRD( C)). This im-plies as an immediate corollary that for any subset of coordinates of C, we can sparsify it to 

eO(BADNRD( C)/ϵ 4) many coordinates, as we desire. To see why BACNRD( C, ϵ ′) = eO(BADNRD( C)), let us consider any subcode Ci with support 

Ai as returned by Definition 4.1. If |Ai| = 1, then there must already be some vector v ∈ Ci such that v|Ai̸ = 0. Otherwise, all we will use is that |Ci|Ai | ≥ 2|Ai|, which is due to the fact that for every choice of B ⊆ Ai, there is a different codeword v ∈ (Ci)|Ai . Now, we can simply invoke Corollary 2.6 to conclude that there must exist symbols b0̸ = b1 ∈ { 0, a 1, . . . a k}, along with a set 

A′ 

> i

⊆ Ai such that |A′

> i

| = eΩ( |Ai|) and {b0, b 1}A′ 

> i

⊆ (Ci)|A′ 

> i

> p

# 6 Sparsifying Continuous Unbounded Codes 

In this section, we consider sparsifying a code C ⊆ Rm

> ≥0

.

Remark 6.1. Because sparsification is unchanged under scaling, we assume without loss of gen-erality that for every vector v ∈ C, max i∈[m] vi = m3. This is because, for any codeword c ∈ C,sparsifying any scaling of the codeword α · c for α > 0 is equivalent to sparsifying c.Note that the choice of having the upper end of the interval at m3 is intentional; during the proof, we will end up “thresholding” around the value 1 as it is sufficiently smaller than m3.Towards this end, we first define the notion of continuous-valued non-redundancy which we will show governs the sparsifiability of these codes: 

Definition 6.1. Let C ⊆ { [0 , m 3]}m, and let ϵ > 0. CVNRD( C, ϵ ) is defined as the maximum ℓ

such that there exists C1, . . . C p ⊆ C along with A1, . . . A p ⊆ [m] such that: 53 1. ℓ = Ppi=1 |Ai|.2. For each i ∈ [p], if |Ai| = 1, Ci is of size 1, and we set b(i)2 = cj > 0 for the single codeword 

c ∈ Ci and single coordinate j ∈ Ai.3. For each i ∈ [p], if |Ai| ≥ 2, there exists b(i)1 , b (i)2 ∈ R, b(i)1 < b (i)2 (1 − ϵ) such that for every

6.1 Warm-Up: CVNRD Gives Random Sampling Sparsification Lower Bounds 

To begin building intuition for the behavior of CVNRD, we start by showing that CVNRD consti-tutes a lower bound for any scheme which produces sparsifiers by random sampling:

6.2 Notation 

To prove our main theorems in this section, we first introduce some new pieces of notation. 

Definition 6.3. For a codeword c ∈ [0 , m 3]m, a value a ∈ [ 11+ ϵ , m 3], and ϵ > 0, we use c(a,ϵ ) to refer to the codeword ∈ { [a · (1 + ϵ)−1, a · (1 + ϵ)2]}m where for i ∈ [m], 

c(a,ϵ ) 

> i

= min(max( ci, a · (1 + ϵ)−1), a · (1 + ϵ)2).

Intuitively, any symbol in c that is less than a · (1 + ϵ)−1 gets rounded up to a · (1 + ϵ)−1, and any symbol greater than a · (1 + ϵ)2 gets rounded down to a · (1 + ϵ)2.57 Definition 6.4. For C ⊆ [0 , m 3]m, a value a ∈ [1 , m 3] and ϵ > 0, and a value β ∈ Z, we use Ca,β,ϵ 

to refer to a code ⊆ { [a · (1 + ϵ)−1, a · (1 + ϵ)2]}m such that: 

Ca,β,ϵ = {c(a,ϵ ) : c ∈ C, |{ i ∈ [m] : ci ∈ [a, (1 + ϵ)a]}| ∈ [β, 2β]}.

In words, this is the code which includes only codewords c in C which have between β and 2 β many symbols with value [ a, (1 + ϵ)a], and then for each such codeword, replaces it with the c(a,ϵ ) version. Now, we partition the codewords in Ca,β,ϵ :

Definition 6.6. For a codeword c ∈ [0 , m 3]m, a value a ∈ [0 , m 3] and a value ϵ > 0, we let: 1. wt a,ϵ (c) = Pmi=1 ci · 1[ci ∈ a · [1 , (1 + ϵ)]]. 2. # a,ϵ (c) = Pmi=1 1[ci ∈ a · [1 , (1 + ϵ)]]. 3. More generally, for a symbol b ∈ [0 , m 3] wt [a,b ](c) = Pmi=1 ci · 1[ci ∈ [a, b ]] and # [a,b ](c) = Pmi=1 1[ci ∈ [a, b ]]. For this section, we will use additive covers instead of multiplicative covers.

58 6.3 Defining the Peeled Sets 

With the above preliminaries established, we are now ready to proceed to a discussion of how we build our sparsifier. As before, our goal, given a code C ⊆ [0 , m 3]m and parameter ϵ > 0, is to peel off a small set of coordinates S ⊆ [m], such that when we sample the remaining coordinates [ m] − S

at rate 1 /2 (and give these sampled coordinates weight 2), the resulting code is a (1 ± ϵ) sparsifier of C.To do this, let us fix a value of a ∈

n 11+ ϵ , 1, (1 + ϵ), . . . m 

> (1+ ϵ)

, m 

o

, and a value of β ∈ { 1, 2, 4, 8, . . . m }.Now, observe that, for a codeword c ∈ C, if wt a,ϵ (c) ≤ ϵ2·wt( c)100 log( m) , then the codeword c is effectively already taken care of: indeed, under random sampling at rate 1 /2, the maximum additive error introduced by symbols in c with value ∈ a · [1 , 1 + ϵ] is at most wt a,ϵ (c), which is a negligible portion of the weight of c. Thus, we instead focus (WLOG) only on codewords c ∈ C where wt a,ϵ (c) ≥ ϵ2·wt( c)100 log( m) .In particular, when we focus our attention on Ca,β,ϵ , this means that we can assume (without loss of generality) that for every codeword c(a,β,ϵ ) ∈ Ca,β,ϵ , that the corresponding codeword c ∈ C

satisfied wt a(c) ≥ ϵ2·wt( c)100 log( m) , and so in particular, 

a · (1 + ϵ) · 2β ≥ wt a(c) ≥ ϵ2 · wt( c)100 log( m) .
