6.5 Building the Sparsifier 

To build our sparsifier for C, we now adopt a simple re-sparsification approach as is typically used in these settings (see [KPS24a, KPS25, BG25] for instance). We outline this algorithm below: 

Algorithm 6: Sparsify( C, ϵ ) 

> 1

i = 0.  

> 2

T (0) = [ m].  

> 3

while |Supp( C|T (i) )| > 0 do  

> 4

Let S(i), T (i+1) be the sets as returned by Lemma 6.22.  

> 5

i ← i + 1.  

> 6

end  

> 7

Let d ← i return d, (S(0) , S (1) , . . . S (d)).

First we bound the number of iterations performed in Algorithm 6: 

Claim 6.24. When Algorithm 6 is invoked on a code C ⊆ [0 , m 3]m with parameter ϵ, the algorithm runs for d ≤ 10 log( m) many iterations. Proof. Indeed, by Lemma 6.22, we know that |T (i+1) | ≤ 23 · | T (i)|. Thus, after d = log 3/2(m) ≤

10 log( m) iterations, the set T (d) must be empty. Now, we prove the accuracy of the aforementioned sparsifier: 

Claim 6.25. When Algorithm 6 is invoked on a code C with parameter ϵ, yielding sets S(0) , . . . S (d) ⊆

[m], it must be the case that for every codeword c ∈ C,



> d

X

> j=0

X

> i∈S(j)

2j · ci

 ∈ (1 ± 5ϵ)10 log( m) ·

 X

> i∈[m]

ci

 .

Proof. We prove the claim inductively. For the base case, observe that via Lemma 6.22, the weight function w : S(0) → 1, w : T (1) → 2 (and otherwise w is 0) is a (1 ± ϵ) sparsifier of C.Now, we claim inductively that for d′ < d , and j ∈ [d′], the weight function w(d′) : S(j) →

2j , w (d′) : T (d′+1) → 2d′+1 and otherwise is 0 is a (1 ± 5ϵ)d′

sparsifier of C. Next, by Lemma 6.22, we know that the weight function w′ : S(d′+1) → 2d′+1 , w ′ : T (d′+2) → 2d′+2 (and otherwise is 0) must be a (1 ± ϵ) sparsifier of 2 d′+1 · C|T (d′+1) , equivalently the code with weight 2 d′+1 on all coordinates in T (d′+1) (and otherwise 0). 79 Thus, we can now compose these sparsifiers. Indeed, we construct the weight function w(d′+1) 

such that for j ∈ [d′ + 1], w(d′+1) : S(j) → 2j , w (d′+1) : T (d′+2) → 2d′+2 . This is exactly the result of replacing w(d′) : T (d′+1) → 2d′+1 with w′. Lemma 6.22 guarantees that this worsens the accuracy of the sparsifier by at most a factor of (1 ± 5ϵ). Thus, we get that the weight function w(d′+1) is a (1 ± ϵ)d′+1 sparsifier of C.By induction then (and using our bound on d from Claim 6.24), we then obtain the stated claim. Finally, we are now ready to conclude this section:

# 7 Applications to Sparsifying Valued CSPs 

As an application, we obtain the first general characterization of the worst-case sparsifiability of 

valued constraint satisfaction problems (VCSPs) , or equivalently, of hypergraphs equipped with arbitrary real-valued splitting functions. Given a predicate P : Σ r → R≥0 and a universe of n

variables x1, . . . x n ∈ Σ, an instance Ψ of a VCSP is then specified by creating m constraints, each of which is the result of applying the predicate P to some subset of r variables (we denote these subsets by T1, . . . T m ∈  [n]

> r

), and re-weighted by a value wi. For an assignment x ∈ Σn, the value 

attained by the CSP is then exactly Ψ( x) = P 

> i∈[m]

wi · P (x|Ti ). CSP sparsification then calls for a re-weighted sub-CSP ˆΨ such that, for every x ∈ Σn,ˆΨ( x) ∈ (1 ± ϵ) · Ψ( x),

80 while ˆΨ retains as few constraints as possible. This exactly matches general hypergraph sparsifi-cation: variables are vertices; each constraint induces a hyperedge with splitting function P ; the alphabet Σ specifies the number of parts of the partition (the standard cut case corresponds to 

|Σ| = 2). Following prior CSP sparsification work [KK15, FK17, BZ20, KPS24a, KPS25, BG25, BGP25, BGP26], we fix a predicate P (with constant |Σ| and arity r) and ask: what is the worst-case size of a (1 ±ϵ)-sparsifier over all n-variable, unweighted instances? We denote this by SPR( P, n, ϵ ). While valued CSPs have previously been considered in real-valued sparsification [BZ20, BG25], concrete structural characterizations were known only for random instances [BGP25]. Most prior work has focused on Boolean predicates. For Boolean P : Σ r → { 0, 1}, CSP spar-sification coincides with {0, 1}-code sparsification by mapping each assignment x to the codeword 

c(x) ∈ { 0, 1}m where c(x)i = 1[P (x|Ti ) = 1]. For general real-valued predicates, our framework naturally suggests an analogous structural parameter. 

Definition 7.1. Let P : Σ r → R≥0 and consider all n-variable instances Ψ with constraints on 

T1, . . . , T m. Define DNRD( P, n ) as the maximum ℓ such that there exist disjoint A1, . . . , A p ⊆ [m]and assignment families X1, . . . , X p ⊆ Σn with Ppi=1 |Ai| = ℓ and: 1. ( Block-diagonal ) For every i̸ = j, for every x ∈ Xi and every ℓ ∈ Aj , P (x|Tℓ ) = 0. 2. ( Singletons ) If |Ai| = 1, then there exists x ∈ Xi with P (x|Tℓ )̸ = 0 for the unique ℓ ∈ Ai.3. ( Completeness ) If |Ai| ≥ 2, there exist b0̸ = b1 ∈ Im( P ) such that for every v ∈ { b0, b 1}Ai

there exists x ∈ Xi with P (x|Tℓ ) = vℓ for all ℓ ∈ Ai.Indeed, by Theorem 5.1, we can immediately conclude that DNRD( P, n ) is essentially equal (up to polylogarithmic factors) to the worst-case sparsifiability of VCSPs with predicate P .

Corollary 7.1. For every predicate P : Σ r → R≥0 and every ϵ ∈ (0 , κ ) for some κ = Ω(1) ,

Ω DNRD( P, n ) ≤ SPR( P, n, ϵ ) ≤ eO DNRD( P, n )/ϵ 4.

For constant ϵ, this corollary provides an exact analog of [BG25]: it reduces the study of VCSP sparsifier size to understanding the redundancy induced by the predicate P . In particular, when complete blocks are absent, VCSP sparsifiability coincides with that of the corresponding {0, 1}-valued CSP (and code). An identical statement holds for weighted sparsifiability when DNRD is replaced by an analog with chain length. Note that it may seem that the above definition is artificial, but it turns out that these “witnesses of unsparsifiability” that are suggested by Definition 7.1 are in fact inherent in the sparsification of VCSPs. To see this, let us study the predicate P : {0, 1}2 → { 0, 1, 2} such that: 

P (00) = 0 , P (01) = 0 , P (10) = 1 , P (11) = 2 .

We have the following claim: 

Claim 7.2. DNRD( P, n ) = Ω( n2).Proof. To see this, let us consider the complete bipartite instance on n variables. In more detail, we let L, R denote two sets of n/ 2 variables, and for each u ∈ L and each v ∈ R, we add a constraint of the form P (xu, x v). Now, for each u ∈ L, we consider the set of assignments Ψ u ∈ { 0, 1}L∪R = {0}L−u × { 1}u ×{0, 1}R, and the set of constraints Au = {P (xu, x v) : v ∈ R}. We show that this set of constraints and assignments satisfies the requirements of Definition 7.1: 81 1. Consider any assignment x ∈ Ψu′ for u̸ = u. Then, for any constraint in Au of the form 

P (xu, x v), we see 

P (xu, x v) = P (0 , x v) = 0 ,

as xu = 0 in any assignment from Ψ u′ . Thus, this collection of assignments satisfies the block-diagonal condition. 2. Note that no set Au is of size 1, and so the singleton condition is trivially satisfied. 3. Otherwise, consider any vector of values φ ∈ { 1, 2}Au . We claim that for each such vector v,there exists an assignment x ∈ Ψu such that for every constraint ( u, v ) ∈ Au,

φ(u,v ) = P (xu, x v).

For this, we simply look at the assignment x such that 

xi =



0 if i ∈ L − u

1 if i = uφ(u,i ) − 1 if i ∈ R.

Indeed, for this x, one can then verify that P (xu, x v) for v ∈ R satisfies 

P (xu, x v) = P (1 , φ (u,v ) − 1) = 

(

2 if φ(u,v ) = 2 1 if φ(u,v ) = 1 .

Thus, this set of constraints and assignments satisfies the complete block condition. To conclude then, the above implies that DNRD( P, n ) ≥ P 

> u∈L

|Au| = n 

> 2

· n 

> 2

= Ω( n2). We then obtain the following as an immediate corollary from Corollary 7.1: 

Corollary 7.3. There exists ϵ = Ω(1) such that SPR( P, n, ϵ ) = Ω( n2).

Remark 7.4. Note that if one replaces the predicate P with its “booleanization” P ′ : {0, 1}r →{0, 1} such that 

P ′(00) = 0 , P ′(01) = 0 , P ′(10) = 1 , P ′(11) = 1 ,

then SPR( P ′, n, ϵ ) = eO(n/ϵ 2) (see, for instance, [KPS24a]). The sparsification lower bound of Corollary 7.3 is inherent to the valued setting. We view it as a very interesting open question to build a more complete characterization of this “discrete” non-redundancy in valued CSPs, akin to the work of [KPS24a, KPS25, BG25, BGJ +25, BGP26]. 

# 8 Applications to Stronger Graph Sparsification 

As an additional application of our framework, we study basic variants of graph sparsification. 82 8.1 Background 

To do so, we first formally introduce the notion of cut sparsification, which dates back to the work of [BK96, Tal90]: 

Definition 8.1 (Cut Sparsification) . Given a graph G = ( V, E ) and an assignment x ∈ RV , we define the cut-energy of G on assignment x to be exactly 

X

> e=( u,v )∈E

|xu − xv|.

For a parameter ϵ > 0, a (1 ± ϵ) cut-sparsifier of G is a re-weighted subset of edges E′ ⊆ E along with weights w : E′ → R≥0 such that for every x ∈ RV ,

 X

> e=( u,v )∈E′

w(e) · | xu − xv|

 ∈ (1 ± ϵ) ·

 X

> e=( u,v )∈E

|xu − xv|

 .

The celebrated works of [BK96, Tal90] show that such cut sparsification is indeed achievable while retaining only eO  n/ϵ 2 many edges from the graph G.In a separate direction, several works have also studied spectral sparsification of graphs (see, for instance, [SS08, BSS09, ST11]): 

Definition 8.2 (Spectral Sparsification) . Given a graph G = ( V, E ) and an assignment x ∈ RV ,we define the spectral-energy of G on assignment x to be exactly 

X

> e=( u,v )∈E

|xu − xv|2.

For a parameter ϵ > 0, a (1 ± ϵ) spectral sparsifier of G is a re-weighted subset of edges E′ ⊆ E

along with weights w : E′ → R≥0 such that for every x ∈ RV ,

 X

Again in this regime, [SS08, BSS09, ST11] have been able to show the existence of sparsifiers which retain only O(n/ϵ 2) many edges. We now introduce a new definition, called p-spectral sparsification: 

Definition 8.3 (p-Spectral Sparsification) . Given a graph G = ( V, E ) a parameter p > 0, and an assignment x ∈ RV , we define the p-spectral-energy of G on assignment x to be exactly 

X

> e=( u,v )∈E

|xu − xv|p.

For a parameter ϵ > 0, a (1 ± ϵ) p-spectral sparsifier of G is a re-weighted subset of edges E′ ⊆ E

83 Note that this notion of energy has implicitly been studied before: indeed, the work of [JLLS23] showed that graphs G = ( V, E ) admit (1 ± ϵ) p-spectral sparsifiers with eO

 max( n,n p/ 2)

> ϵ2

many retained edges. However, for p > 2, this result loses polynomial factors in n in the sparsifier size (and for p ≥ 4, it is completely trivial). In this section, we prove the following strengthening of their result: 

Theorem 8.1. Let G = ( V, E ) be a graph on n vertices, let ϵ > 0, and let p ≥ 1. Then, G admits a (1 ± ϵ) p-spectral sparsifier of size O (n · poly(log p(n), ϵ −p)) .

This theorem shows that, among others, when p is constant (i.e., p = O(1)), there exists a 

p-spectral sparsifier which retains only eO  n · poly( ϵ−1) many edges. 

8.2 Notation and Connection to CVNRD 

To prove Theorem 8.1, we first require the notion of the “ p-spectral code” of a graph: 

Definition 8.4. Let G = ( V, E ) be a graph (potentially a multi-graph) on n vertices and m

edges. We let CG,p ⊆ Rm 

> ≥0

denote the p-spectral code of G which contains one vector v(x) for each assignment x ∈ RV , where for an edge e = ( u, v ) ∈ E, the corresponding entry in the vector v(x) is (v(x))e = |xu − xv|p.

Remark 8.2. Observe that, for a graph G, building a (1 ± ϵ) code sparsifier of CG,p is in fact 

equivalent to building a (1 ± ϵ) p-spectral sparsifier of the graph G.Inspired by this connection to CG,p , we now define the CVNRD of a graph G:

Definition 8.5. For a graph G = ( V, E ), and parameters ϵ > 0, χ > 0, ρ > 0, p ≥ 1, the continuous-valued non-redundancy of G’s p-spectral energy (denoted by CVNRD p(G, ϵ, χ, ρ )) is defined as the largest integer ℓ such that there exist disjoint sets of edges F1, . . . b(i)2 · [1 , 1 + χ · ϵ], and for e = ( u, v ) ∈ Fi − B, |xu − xv|p ∈ b(i)1 · [1 , 1 + χ · ϵ]. 4. For each Fi : i ∈ [k], and for every x ∈ Ψi, P

# 9 Applications to Stronger Hypergraph Sparsification 

Just as our framework can be used to study variants of graph sparsification, the same approach works for studying hypergraph sparsification. As in the previous section, we begin with some background about hypergraph sparsification.

Theorem 9.1. Let H = ( V, E ) be a hypergraph on n vertices and m hyperedges, let ϵ > 0, and let 

p ≥ 1. Then, G admits a (1 ± ϵ) p-spectral sparsifier of size O (n · poly(log p(m), ϵ −p)) .

This theorem shows that, among others, when p is constant (i.e., p = O(1)), there exists a 

p-spectral sparsifier which retains only eO  n · poly( ϵ−1) many hyperedges. 91 9.2 Notation and Connection to CVNRD 

To prove Theorem 9.1, we first require the notion of the “ p-spectral code” of a graph: 

Definition 9.4. Let H = ( V, E ) be a hypergraph on n vertices and m hyperedges. We let CH,p ⊆

Rm 

> ≥0

denote the p-spectral code of H which contains one vector v(x) for each assignment x ∈ RV ,where for a hyperedge e ∈ E, the corresponding entry in the vector v(x) is ( v(x))e = max u,v ∈e |xu −

xv|p.

Remark 9.2. Observe that, for a hypergraph H, building a (1 ± ϵ) code sparsifier of CH,p is in fact equivalent to building a (1 ± ϵ) p-spectral sparsifier of the hypergraph H.Inspired by this connection to CH,p , we now define the CVNRD of a hypergraph H:

Definition 9.5. For a hypergraph H = ( V, E ), and parameters ϵ > 0, χ > 0, ρ > 0, p ≥ 1, the continuous-valued non-redundancy of H’s p-spectral energy (denoted by CVNRD p(H, ϵ, χ, ρ )) is defined as the largest integer ℓ such that there exist disjoint sets of edges F1, . . . F k ⊆ E, along with sets of potential assignments Ψ 1, . . . Ψk ∈ RV such that: 1. Pki=1 |Fi| = ℓ.2. For i ∈ [k], if |Fi| = 1, then let b(i)2 = max u,v ∈e |xu − xv|p > 0 for the unique assignment 

x ∈ Ψi, and hyperedge e ∈ Fi.3. For each Fi : i ∈ [k], there exist values b(i)1 , b (i)2 , with b(i)1 < b (i)2 (1 − ϵ), such that for any set of edges B ⊆ Fi, there is an assignment x ∈ Ψi such that for e ∈ B, max u,v ∈e |xu − xv|p ∈

b(i)2 · [1 , 1 + χ · ϵ], and for e = ( u, v ) ∈ Fi − B, max u,v ∈e |xu − xv|p ∈ b(i)1 · [1 , 1 + χ · ϵ]. 4. For each Fi : i ∈ [k], and for every x ∈ Ψi, P 

> j∈[k]: j̸=i

Proposition 9.3. For a hypergraph H = ( V, E ), and parameters ϵ > 0, χ > 0, ρ > 0, p ≥ 1,CVNRD (CH,p , ϵ, χ, ρ ) = CVNRD p(H, ϵ, χ, ρ ).

Proof. Any witness of CVNRD( CH,p , ϵ, χ, ρ ) can be directly translated into a witness of CVNRD p(H, ϵ, χ, ρ )and vice versa. 

9.3 A Connection to Graph-Theoretic Sauer-Shelah 

In order to streamline the proof of Theorem 9.1, we use a result of [CBH98]. As in their work, we let N denote a finite set of symbols, and we let G = ( N, R ) be an undirected graph over 

N . The mth power of G, denoted Gm, has vertex set N m and edge set Rm, where an edge 

{(u1, . . . u m), (v1, . . . v m)} ∈ Rm if there exists an index i ∈ [m] such that {ui, v i} ∈ R.For a subset A = {i1, . . . i ℓ} ⊆ [m], and a set F ⊆ N m, we say that the projection of F onto A

is 

F |A = {(vi1 , . . . v iℓ ) : ( v1, . . . v m) ∈ F }.

A set Q ⊆ N m is a cube in Gm if Q = {u1, v 1} × { u2, v 2} × · · · × { um, v m}, where each {ui, v i} ∈ R.We say that A, Q is a d-dimensional projected cube of a set F ⊆ V m if A ⊆ [m], |A| = d, and 

Q ⊆ F |A is a d-dimensional cube in Gd.92 Lastly, [CBH98] defines h(G, m, d ) to be the smallest non-negative integer h such that, every clique F in Gm with |F | > h contains a ( d+1)-cube. The main theorem of [CBH98] is the following: 

Theorem 9.4. For any undirected graph G = ( N, R ) and any m > d ≥ 0,

h(G, m, d ) < 2 · (2 m|R|)⌈log Pdi=0 (mi )|R|i⌉.

In particular, one consequence of the above theorem is the following: 

Corollary 9.5. Let C ⊆ { 0, 1, 2, . . . N − 1}m be such that, for every subset B ⊆ [m], there is a codeword c ∈ C such that, for j ∈ B, cj = 0 , and for j ∈ [m] − B, cj̸ = 0 .Then, there exists a subset A ⊆ [m], |A| ≥ m−1log(2 mN )2 along with a set of symbols ai : i ∈ A

such that, for every set B′ ⊆ A, there is a codeword c ∈ C such that for j ∈ B′, cj = 0 , and for 

j ∈ A − B′, cj = aj .Proof. We define the graph G = ( N, R ) with the understanding that N is in correspondence with the symbols {0, 1, 2, . . . N − 1}. The edges R are then exactly {0, i } : i ∈ { 1, 2, . . . N − 1}.Next, we observe that C is a clique in Gm: indeed, consider any two vectors c, c ′ ∈ C, where c

witnesses set B and c′ witnesses set B′. Because B̸ = B′ we let j ∈ B∆B′. For this index j, we then see that exactly one of cj , c ′ 

> j

is 0, and the other takes value ̸ = 0. Thus, ( cj , c j′ ) ∈ R, and so 

c, c ′ have an edge in Gm.Thus, we have a clique of size 2 m in Gm. By Theorem 9.4, for any d such that 2m ≥ 2 · (2 m|R|)⌈log Pdi=0 (mi )|R|i⌉,

we then know that there must be a cube of dimension d inside C. In particular, using R = N , we can then choose d such that

(4 p log( n)) p · 2



= O(n log 2(mn )) .

> 8

Note that the conditions we have on the graph are now weaker; notably the lower energy edges only satisfy 

|xu − xv |p ≤ b(i)1 · (1 + χ · ϵ) as opposed to |xu − xv |p ∈ b(i)1 · (1 , 1 + χ · ϵ), and we have no bound on the sum of off-diagonal energies. Neither of these stronger conditions are used in the proof of Theorem 8.4. 

96 Thus, SPR( CH,p , ϵ ) = O

 n

 1100 log 2(m)

6  (ϵ′)p 

> (4 plog( m)) p·2

 · poly(log( m), ϵ −1)



= O  n · poly(log p(m), ϵ −p) .

This concludes the theorem. 

# 10 Applications to Bounded Submodular Sparsification 

In this section, we prove Theorem 1.5. Recall that in this setting, we are given a universe of n

elements, and m submodular functions f1, . . . f m : 2 [n] → { 0, 1, 2, . . . k }, for some k = O(1). Our goal is to compute a (1 ± ϵ) sparsifier of this collection of submodular functions. We show: 

Theorem 10.1. Let k = O(1) be an integer, and let f1, . . . f m : 2 [n] → { 0, 1, 2, 3, . . . k } be submod-ular functions. Then, there exists T ⊆ [m] along with weights w : T → R≥0 such that for every 

S ⊆ [n], X

> i∈T

w(i) · fi(S) ∈ (1 ± ϵ) · X

> i∈[m]

fi(S),

and |T | = eO(n2/ϵ 4).

To start, observe that given this collection of submodular functions, we can define an equivalent code C ⊆ { 0, 1, 2 . . . k }m such that C contains a codeword for each S ⊆ [n], where 

c(S) 

> i

= fi(S).

Under this mapping, any (1 ± ϵ) sparsifier of C is inherently a (1 ± ϵ) sparsifier of f1, . . . f m

(and vice versa). By Theorem 5.1, we know that the sparsifiability of this code C is bounded by 

eO(BADNRD( C)/ϵ 4), where BADNRD( C) is defined as the maximum integer ℓ such that there exists A1, . . . A p ⊆ [m] and C1, . . . C p ⊆ C, with Ppi=1 |Ai| = ℓ, and: 1. For every i ∈ [p], if |Ai| = 1, then ( Ci)|Ai ∈ { 1, 2, . . . k }.2. For every i ∈ [p], if |Ai| ≥ 2, then ( Ci)|Ai = {b0, b 1}|Ai| for some {b0, b 1} ⊆ { 0, 1, . . . k }.3. For every j̸ = i, ( Ci)|Aj = 0 Aj .Thus, the remainder of this section focuses on bounded the maximum size “BADNRD witness” that can be constructed within any collection of submodular functions. Formally, we show: 

Theorem 10.2. Let 

f1, . . . , f m : 2 [n] → { 0, 1, 2, . . . k }

be nonnegative submodular functions which constitute a BADNRD witness. I.e., that [m] admits a partition into A1, . . . A p, along with collections of sets S1, . . . Sp ⊆ 2[n] such that: 1. For every i ∈ [p], if |Ai| = 1 , then for j ∈ Ai, and for every S ∈ S i, fj (S) ∈ { 1, 2, . . . k }.2. For every i ∈ [p], if |Ai| ≥ 2, then there exists {ai, b i} ⊆ { 0, 1, . . . k } such that for any 

v ∈ { ai, b i}Ai , there exists S ∈ S i such that for every j ∈ Ai, fj (S) = vj .

97 3. For every i′̸ = i, for every S ∈ S i and every j ∈ Ai′ , fj (S) = 0 .Then, 

m ≤ n2 + n. 

Remark 10.3. As an immediate consequence, Theorem 10.2 implies that BADNRD( C) ≤ n2 + n

for any code C which is generated as the evaluations of submodular functions f1, . . . f m.

10.1 Notation 

We identify points of {0, 1}n with subsets of [ n]. Recall that a set function 

f : 2 [n] → R

is submodular if, for every S, T ⊆ [n], 

f (S) + f (T ) ≥ f (S ∩ T ) + f (S ∪ T ).

10.2 Zero Sets of Nonnegative Submodular Functions 

To start, we recap basic properties of the behvaior of 0 sets of submodular functions. This will be of particular importance to us because the definition of BADNRD enforces that submodular functions always evaluate to 0 on the “off-diagonal” terms of the witness. A family L ⊆ 2[n] is called a sublattice if it is closed under union and intersection. 

Lemma 10.4. Let 

f : 2 [n] → R≥0

be submodular. Then the zero set 

Z(f ) := {S ⊆ [n] : f (S) = 0 }

is a sublattice of 2[n].Proof. Let S, T ∈ Z(f ). Then 

f (S) = f (T ) = 0 .

By submodularity, 0 = f (S) + f (T ) ≥ f (S ∩ T ) + f (S ∪ T ).

Both terms on the right are nonnegative, so both must be zero. Hence 

S ∩ T ∈ Z(f ) and S ∪ T ∈ Z(f ).

Thus Z(f ) is closed under intersections and unions. We will repeatedly use the following immediate consequence. 

Lemma 10.5. Let 

f : 2 [n] → R≥0

be submodular, and suppose f (A) = 0 . Then for every S ⊆ [n],

f (S ∩ A) ≤ f (S) and f (S ∪ A) ≤ f (S).

Proof. By submodularity, 

f (S) + f (A) ≥ f (S ∩ A) + f (S ∪ A).

Since f (A) = 0, this gives 

f (S) ≥ f (S ∩ A) + f (S ∪ A).

Both terms on the right are nonnegative, so each is at most f (S). 98 10.3 Canonical Data of a Sublattice 

Now, in order to prove our bound on the size of any BADNRD witness, we need a proxy to quantify the “degrees of freedom” of submodular functions. We quantify this with the following definitions. Let L ⊆ 2[n] be a nonempty sublattice. Define its common core by 

CL := \

> A∈L

A. 

Thus CL is the set of elements that appear in every member of L. Define its support by 

UL := [

> A∈L

A. 

Thus UL is the set of elements that appear in at least one member of L. Finally, for each u ∈ [n], define 

ML(u) := \

> A∈L
> u∈A

A. 

If no member of L contains u, then we use the convention 

ML(u) = [ n].

When u ∈ UL, the set ML(u) belongs to L and is the smallest member of L containing u.We now define a finite list of possible defects of a set S relative to L; these will essentially be our “degress of freedom” going forward. Let Γn := {αv : v ∈ [n]} ∪ { βu : u ∈ [n]} ∪ { γu,v : u, v ∈ [n], u ̸ = v}.

Thus 

|Γn| = n + n + n(n − 1) = n2 + n. 

For S ⊆ [n], define Def L(S) ⊆ Γn

as follows. First, αv ∈ Def L(S) if and only if v ∈ CL \ S. This records that S is missing an element that every member of L contains. Second, βu ∈ Def L(S) if and only if u ∈ S \ UL. This records that S contains an element that no member of L contains. Third, γu,v ∈ Def L(S) if and only if 

u ∈ S, v ∈ ML(u) \ S, u̸ = v. 

This records that S contains u but fails to contain some element that is always present in the smallest member of L containing u. The next lemma shows that these defects exactly describe membership in L.

Lemma 10.6. Let L ⊆ 2[n] be a nonempty sublattice. For S ⊆ [n],

S ∈ L 

if and only if 

Def L(S) = ∅.

99 Proof. First suppose S ∈ L . Since CL is the intersection of all members of L, we have CL ⊆ S. 

Thus no defect of type αv occurs. Likewise, because UL is the union of all members of L, we have 

S ⊆ UL. Thus no defect of type βu occurs. Finally, if u ∈ S, then S is one of the members of L containing u. Therefore ML(u) ⊆ S. Thus no defect of type γu,v occurs. Hence Def L(S) = ∅.

Conversely, suppose Def L(S) = ∅. Then CL ⊆ S and S ⊆ UL. If S = ∅, then CL = ∅. Since CL

is an intersection of members of L and L is intersection-closed, we have 

∅ = CL ∈ L .

Now suppose S̸ = ∅. Since S ⊆ UL, for every u ∈ S there is at least one member of L containing 

u, so 

ML(u) ∈ L .

Since no defect of type γu,v occurs, we have 

ML(u) ⊆ S for every u ∈ S. 

Define 

W := [

> u∈S

ML(u).

Because L is union-closed, W ∈ L . Each ML(u) is contained in S, so W ⊆ S. On the other hand, 

u ∈ ML(u) for every u ∈ S, so S ⊆ W. Therefore W = S, and hence S ∈ L .

10.4 A Domination Lemma 

The next lemma shows that these defects exactly capture the ability to translate a set S into a set 

T purely via intersections and unions with the sub-lattice L:

Lemma 10.7. Let L ⊆ 2[n] be a nonempty sublattice, and let S, T ⊆ [n]. Suppose Def L(T ) ⊆ Def L(S).

Then there exist sets 

A ∈ L ∪ {∅} and B ∈ L ∪ { [n]}

such that 

T = A ∪ (S ∩ B).

Moreover, if A = ∅, then the operation of union with A is unnecessary; if B = [ n], then the operation of intersection with B is unnecessary. Proof. Let 

X := T \ S and Y := T ∩ S. 

We first build the part of T outside S.Suppose u ∈ X. Then u ∈ T and u / ∈ S. If u / ∈ UL, then βu ∈ Def L(T ). But since u / ∈ S, we have βu /∈ Def L(S) contradicting Def L(T ) ⊆ Def L(S). Therefore every u ∈ X lies in UL, and so 

ML(u) ∈ L .We claim that 

ML(u) ⊆ T for every u ∈ X. 

100 Indeed, if some v ∈ ML(u) were not in T , then u ∈ T and v / ∈ T , so γu,v ∈ Def L(T ). Here v̸ = u

because u ∈ T . But u / ∈ S, so γu,v /∈ Def L(S), again a contradiction. If X̸ = ∅, define A := S 

> u∈X

ML(u). Then A ∈ L , because L is union-closed. Also X ⊆ A ⊆ T. 

If X = ∅, set A := ∅.

We now build the part of T inside S. First suppose that Y contains some element u / ∈ UL.Since u / ∈ UL, no member of L contains u, and therefore ML(u) = [ n]. We claim that in this case 

S ⊆ T. Indeed, if there existed v ∈ S \ T , then v ∈ ML(u) \ T , and since u ∈ T , we would have 

γu,v ∈ Def L(T ). But u ∈ S and v ∈ S, so γu,v /∈ Def L(S), which is a contradiction. Hence S ⊆ T .In this case set B := [ n]. Then S ∩ B = S = T ∩ S = Y. Now suppose that every element of Y

lies in UL. If Y̸ = ∅, define B := S 

> u∈Y

ML(u). Then B ∈ L . Clearly Y ⊆ S ∩ B. We claim that the reverse containment also holds. Let v ∈ S ∩ B. Then for some u ∈ Y , v ∈ ML(u). If v / ∈ T ,then u ∈ T and v / ∈ T , so γu,v ∈ Def L(T ). But u ∈ S and v ∈ S, so γu,v /∈ Def L(S), which is a contradiction. Therefore v ∈ T , and hence S ∩ B ⊆ T ∩ S = Y. Thus S ∩ B = Y .Finally, suppose Y = ∅. Define B := CL. Then B ∈ L . We claim that S ∩ B = ∅. If some 

v ∈ S ∩ CL existed, then v / ∈ T because Y = T ∩ S = ∅. Hence αv ∈ Def L(T ). But v ∈ S, so 

αv /∈ Def L(S), which is a contradiction. Therefore S ∩ B = ∅ = Y. 

In all cases, we have constructed A and B such that 

X ⊆ A ⊆ T

and 

S ∩ B = Y. 

Therefore 

A ∪ (S ∩ B) = A ∪ Y. 

Since X ⊆ A and A ⊆ T = X ∪ Y , this union is exactly T . Hence T = A ∪ (S ∩ B).

We now combine this representation with submodularity. 

Lemma 10.8 (Submodular domination from defect containment) . Let 

f : 2 [n] → R≥0

be submodular. Let L ⊆ 2[n] be a nonempty sublattice such that f (A) = 0 for every A ∈ L . Then, for any S, T ⊆ [n] such that Def L(T ) ⊆ Def L(S),

it must be that f (T ) ≤ f (S).

Proof. By Lemma 10.7, there are 

A ∈ L ∪ {∅} and B ∈ L ∪ { [n]}

such that T = A ∪ (S ∩ B).

If B ∈ L , then f (B) = 0, and Lemma 10.5 gives f (S ∩ B) ≤ f (S). If B = [ n], then S ∩ B = S, 

so again f (S ∩ B) ≤ f (S).

Now consider A. If A ∈ L , then f (A) = 0, and Lemma 10.5 gives f (A ∪ (S ∩ B)) ≤ f (S ∩ B). If 

A = ∅, then no union operation is needed, and A∪(S ∩B) = S ∩B. In either case, f (T ) ≤ f (S).

The above immediately implies that if two sets share the same defects, then they must in fact be equal under a function f .

Corollary 10.9 (Constancy on equal defect sets) . Let f : 2 [n] → R≥0 be submodular. Let L ⊆ 2[n]

be a nonempty sublattice such that f (A) = 0 for every A ∈ L . If Def L(S) = Def L(T ), then 

f (S) = f (T ).

101 10.5 Concluding the Upper Bound 

Finally, we now prove Theorem 10.2. 

Proof of Theorem 10.2. Discard empty blocks. If only one block remains, say A1 = [ m], then the shattering condition requires 2 m distinct value patterns to be realized by points of 2 [n]. Since the domain has size 2 n, we get 2m ≤ 2n,

and hence 

m ≤ n ≤ n2 + n. 

Thus, we assume there are at least two nonempty blocks. For every block Ai and every subset 

B ⊆ Ai, choose one witnessing set Si,B ⊆ [n] such that 

fj (Si,B ) = ai (j ∈ B),fj (Si,B ) = bi (j ∈ Ai \ B),

and 

fj (Si,B ) = 0 (j / ∈ Ai).

Let 

Wi := {Si,B : B ⊆ Ai}.

For each block Ai, define 

L−i := 

* [

> h̸=i

Wh

+

,

the smallest sublattice containing all witnesses from blocks other than i. Note that since there are at least two nonempty blocks, each L−i is nonempty. Now, fix i and j ∈ Ai. By the shattering condition, 

fj (S) = 0 for every S ∈ W h, h ̸ = i. 

By Lemma 10.4, the zero set of fj is a sublattice. Therefore it contains the sublattice generated by all these outside witnesses. Hence 

fj (S) = 0 for every S ∈ L −i.

For each block i, define its set of block defects by 

Di := [

> B⊆Ai

Def L−i (Si,B ) ⊆ Γn.

We first show that |Ai| ≤ | Di| for every i. Indeed, as S ranges over Wi, every defect set Def L−i (S) is a subset of Di. Therefore there are at most 2 |Di| possible defect sets among the witnesses in Wi.For every j ∈ Ai, the function fj vanishes on L−i. Thus Corollary 10.9 says that if two witnesses 

S, S ′ ∈ W i have the same defect set relative to L−i, then fj (S) = fj (S′) for every j ∈ Ai. Hence the full value vector  fj (S)

> j∈Ai

is determined by the defect set Def L−i (S).

102 On the other hand, the block-shattering condition says that all vectors in {ai, b i}Ai occur. Since 

ai̸ = bi, there are exactly 2 |Ai| such vectors. Therefore 2|Ai| ≤ 2|Di|.

Thus 

|Ai| ≤ | Di|.

Finally, it remains to prove that the sets Di are pairwise disjoint. So, suppose i̸ = h. First consider a label of the form αv. If αv ∈ Di, then there is some witness S ∈ W i such that v ∈ CL−i

and v / ∈ S. The condition v ∈ CL−i means that every set in L−i contains v. In particular, every witness in Wh contains v, because Wh ⊆ L −i. Therefore no witness from Wh can certify αv ∈ Dh.Hence 

αv /∈ Dh.

Next consider a label of the form βu. If βu ∈ Di, then some witness S ∈ W i contains u, while 

u / ∈ UL−i . The latter condition means that no set in L−i contains u. Since Wh ⊆ L −i, no witness in Wh contains u. Therefore βu /∈ Dh.

Finally consider a label of the form γu,v . If γu,v ∈ Di, then there is some witness S ∈ W i such that 

u ∈ S, v / ∈ S, v ∈ ML−i (u).

The condition v ∈ ML−i (u) means that every member of L−i containing u also contains v. Since 

Wh ⊆ L −i, every witness in Wh that contains u must also contain v. Therefore no witness in Wh

can certify γu,v ∈ Dh. Hence 

γu,v /∈ Dh.

Thus 

Di ∩ Dh = ∅ for all i̸ = h. 

Since the Di are pairwise disjoint subsets of Γ n, we have 

X

> i

|Di| ≤ | Γn| = n2 + n. 

Combining this with |Ai| ≤ | Di| for every i, we obtain 

m = X

> i

|Ai| ≤ X

> i

|Di| ≤ n2 + n. 

This proves the theorem.

# A Sparsifying Weighted Continuous Codes With Bounded Aspect Ratio 

In this section, we show how to sparsify weighted continuous codes with bounded aspect ratio to their real-valued chain length . As before, we will focus on codes C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m. Recall that in this situation, the real-valued chain length is defined as: 

Definition A.1. Let C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m. The real-valued chain-length of C with parameter ϵ,denoted by BACCL( C, ϵ ), is the maximum integer ℓ such that there exists A1, . . . A p ⊆ [m] and 

C1, . . . C p ⊆ C, with Ppi=1 |Ai| = ℓ, and: 1. For every i ∈ [p], if |Ai| = 1, then ( Ci)|Ai̸ = {0}.2. For every i ∈ [p] if |Ai| ≥ 2, then there is a choice of vector γ ∈ [1 , k ]Ai , such that for every 

B ⊆ Ai, there is a codeword c ∈ Ci such that for b ∈ B, vb ≥ γb + ϵ, and for b ∈ Ai − B,

vb ≤ γb − ϵ.107 3. For every j > i , ( Ci)|Aj = 0 Aj .For this definition, we have the following characterization: 

Theorem A.1. Let C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m with k = O(1) and let ϵ > 0. Then, 1. There exists ϵ′ such that ϵ′ = Ω( ϵ) and WS( C, ϵ ′) = Ω( ϵ · BACCL( C, ϵ )) .2. For ϵ′ = ϵ/ 256 log( m), WS( C, ϵ ) = eO(BACCL( C, ϵ ′)/ϵ 2).

A.1 Preliminaries 

BACCL satisfies many convenient properties, similar to CL. Importantly, we require the notion of contraction: 

Definition A.2. For a code C ⊆ Rm and a set S ⊆ [m], Contract( C, S ) = {c ∈ C : c|S = 0 }.

To start, we will take advantage of the following claim which governs how BACCL compounds in contracted instances(note that here we use BACCL( C) to mean BACCL( C, ϵ ), where here ϵ can be arbitrarily specified): 

Claim A.2. Let C ⊆ Rm, and let S ⊆ [m]. Then, 

BACCL( C) ≥ BACCL(Contract( C, S )) + BACCL( C|S ).

Proof. Let D = Contract( C, S ), let A1, . . . A ℓ, ( C|S )1, . . . (C|S )ℓ denote the witnesses of the BACCL of C|S , and let A′

Thus, we can create a new witness for the chain length of C by considering the sequence of sets {A′

Remark A.3. Immediately, we can observe that BACCL( C) ≥ CL( ˆC), as any witness to a CL lower bound in ˆC is also a valid witness to BACCL. Likewise, BACNRD( C) ≤ BACCL( C), as any witness to BACNRD is automatically a witness to BACCL. We now proceed to prove the aforementioned lower bound. 108 A.2 Lower Bound

weighted codes. 112 A.3.2 Sparsifying Codes With Polynomially-Bounded Weights 

In the preceding section, we showed how to construct a sparsifier on unweighted instances of size 

eO(BACCL( C, ϵ/ 256 log( m)) /ϵ 2). However, ultimately, our goal was to provide a characterization of 

weighted sparsifiability. As a building block in this direction, in this section, we focus on sparsifying codes whose weights are polynomially bounded. We present the main lemma below: 

Lemma A.6. Let C ⊆ ({0}∪ [1 , k ]) m be a code such that every coordinate has weight ∈ [Wmin , W max ].Then, for any ϵ > 0, C admits a (1 ± ϵ) sparsifier of size eO(BACCL( C, ϵ/ 256 log( m)) · polylog( m ·

Wmax /(Wmin ϵ)) /ϵ 2).Proof. Our strategy is simple: we will just duplicate coordinates a number of times proportional to their weight, and then sparsify this now unweighted code. WLOG, we assume that Wmin = 1, as otherwise, we can simply divide all weights by Wmin , and then at the end multiply all weights by this factor of Wmin .Our key claim is the following: 

Claim A.7. Let C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m be a weighted code with all weights ≥ 1, let ϵ ∈ (0 , 1) , and let 

Cunweighted be the result of duplicating each coordinate i ⌊10 wi/ϵ ⌋ times. Then, for any (1 ± ϵ/ 10) 

sparsifier of Cunweighted (denoted eCunweighted ), ϵ 

> 10

· eCunweighted is a (1 ± ϵ/ 10) 2 sparsifier of C.Proof. Consider any codeword c ∈ C. We use wt( c) to denote the weight of this codeword in C,and we use wt unweighted (c) to denote the corresponding weight of c in Cunweighted . Now, let us fix a single coordinate i ∈ [m]. We will compare the contribution of the weight from ci in C to the contribution of the weight from ci in Cunweighted .Indeed, in C, the contribution from coordinate i is exactly ci ·wi. In Cunweighted , the contribution from coordinate i becomes ci · ⌊ 10 wi/ϵ ⌋. In particular, we can see that (ϵ/ 10) · ci · ⌊ 10 wi/ϵ ⌋ ≥ (ϵ/ 10) · ci · (10 wi/ϵ − 1) ≥ wici − ϵc i/10 .

At the same time, (ϵ/ 10) · ci · ⌊ 10 wi/ϵ ⌋ ≤ wi · ci,

and so all together, we have that (ϵ/ 10) · ci · ⌊ 10 wi/ϵ ⌋ ∈ (1 ± ϵ/ 10) · wici,

where we are using that wi ≥ 1. In particular, this means that for every coordinate, the contribution from the copies of the coordinate in ϵ 

> 10

· eCunweighted is within a (1 ± ϵ/ 10) factor of the contribution in C, so summing across all coordinates means that ϵ 

> 10

· eCunweighted is a (1 ± ϵ/ 10) sparsifier of C. The stated claim above then follows by composition of the sparsification bound. To conclude the lemma, we simply invoke Claim A.7. The resulting unweighted code has at most m · Wmax /(Wmin ϵ) many coordinates, and the BACCL is unchanged, as we have simply duplicated rows. Thus, we can construct a (1 ± ϵ/ 10) sparsifier of Cunweighted which preserves only 

O(BACCL( C)·polylog( m·Wmax /W min ϵ)/ϵ 2) many coordinates, and is in turn a (1 ±ϵ/ 10) 2 ∈ (1 ±ϵ)sparsifier of C by using Lemma A.5. 113 A.3.3 Sparsifying Codes With Unbounded Weights 

Now, we perform a weight class decomposition trick to break the code down into groups, where in each group, the ratios of coordinate weights are bounded. We then invoke Lemma A.6 to sparsify each one of these groups. We introduce this procedure formally below: 

Definition A.3. Let C ⊆ ({0} ∪ [1 , k ]) m, let wi ≥ 1 : i ∈ [m] be a set of weights for the coordinates of C, and let ϵ be the desired accuracy for the resulting sparsifier. We let γ = (1000 mk/ϵ )3, and then define C[γa,γ a+1 ] to be the code C|T [γa,γ a+1 ] , where T [γa,γ a+1 ] = {i ∈ [m] : wi ∈ [γa, γ a+1 ]. We let the code Ceven = S 

> a∈Z≥0

C|T [γ2a,γ 2a+1 ] , and we let Codd = S 

> a∈Z≥0

C|T [γ2a+1 ,γ 2a+2 ] .Going forward, we focus without loss of generality on creating a sparsifier of Ceven . A similar argument will allow us to create a sparsifier of Codd , from which we can then take the union of the two sparsifiers. Now, we require the notion of the largest non-empty weight class :

Definition A.4. Given the code Ceven as defined above, we let Csup ,1 denote the largest non-empty weight class in Ceven . I.e., Csup ,1 = C|T [γ2a, 2a+1 ] for the maximum a ∈ Z≥0 such that 

C|T [γ2a, 2a+1 ] is non-empty. We let T (1) = Supp( Csup ,1), and we let C(even ,2) = Contract( Ceven , T (1) ). We then define Csup ,2 to be the largest non-empty weight class in C(even ,2) , T (2) = Supp( Csup ,2), 

C(even ,3) = Contract( Ceven , T (1) ∪ T (2) ), and so on. Note that the above procedure will terminate, as in each round, we contract on some non-trivial subset of the coordinates. We let t denote the number of iterations in the above procedure. The key structural lemma we will use is the following: 

Lemma A.8. Let C(even ,1) , . . . C (even ,t ) be defined as in Definition A.4, and let ^C(even ,1) , ^C(even ,t ) be 

(1 ± ϵ/ 10) sparsifiers of C(even ,1) , . . . C (even ,t ) respectively. Then, Stj=1 ^C(even ,j ) is a (1 ± ϵ) sparsifier of Ceven .Proof. Consider any codeword c ∈ Ceven , and let j∗ ∈ [t] denote the first round where c|T (j∗)̸ = 0. Importantly, because j∗ is the first round where this support is non-zero, it must be the case that 

c|T (<j ∗) = 0. Additionally, because in our contraction procedure, we only delete codewords which are non-zero , this means that c has not been removed from the code by round j∗, i.e., c ∈ C(sup ,j ∗).Now, by the lemma statement, we know that ^C(even ,j ∗) is a (1 ± ϵ/ 10) sparsifier of C(sup ,j ∗)|T (j∗) ,which in particular means that wt( c|T (j∗) ) is preserved to a (1 ± ϵ/ 10) factor. The remaining coordinates in the support of c must be in lower level weight classes. The intuition is that these weight classes can have only a negligible impact on the weight assigned to c

by the sparsifier, and indeed this will be the case. First, we can observe that wt( c|T (j∗) )wt( c) = wt( c|T (j∗) )wt( c|T (j∗) ) + wt( c|T (>j ∗) ) ≥ (1000 mk/ϵ )3ℓ∗

Importantly, for all coordinates in T (>j ∗), all coordinates have weight ≤ γℓ∗−1.114 Now, because ^C(even ,j ∗) is a (1 ± ϵ/ 10) sparsifier of C(sup ,j ∗)|T (j∗) , this means that 

fwt( c) ≥ fwt( c|T (j∗) )) ≥ (1 − ϵ/ 10) · wt( c|T (j∗) ) ≥ (1 − ϵ/ 10) · (1 − ϵ/ 100)wt( c) ≥ (1 − ϵ)wt( c).

So, it remains only to show the upper bound on the sparsifier weight. Here, we start by remarking, for every j ∈ [t], because ^C(even ,j ) is a (1 ± ϵ/ 10) sparsifier of C(sup ,j )|T (j) , if we let ℓ

denote the weight class of C(even ,j ) (i.e., the weights are of size γℓ,ℓ +1 ), then it must be the case that the weights assigned by ^C(even ,j ) are ≤ 8mk 2 · γℓ+1 . This is because if we assume for the sake of contradiction that some wi : i ∈ T (j) is given such a large weight, then there is some codeword y ∈ C(even ,j ), for which yi ≥ 1, whose weight is now reported to be fwt( yi) ≥ 8mk 2 · γℓ+1 .But, because y ∈ C(even ,j ), Supp( y) is only defined on coordinates of weight ≤ γℓ+1 , and thus wt( y) ≤ mk · γℓ+1 . Thus, any sparsifier assigning a weight wi ≥ 8mk 2 · γℓ+1 is not a (1 ± ϵ/ 10) sparsifier for the codeword y.With this in hand, we can now see that
